**Review.** Same-source preliminary acceptance uses all six named comparisons [Lean]. The connecting maps are transported as existential witnesses. The other output of the same consumer is the Sha transport of [§18.3.17](#18317-transporting-the-tateshafarevich-kernels-in-degrees-one-and-two).

### 18.3.17 Transporting the Tate–Shafarevich kernels in degrees one and two

**Motivation.** A global cohomology class can be nonzero even when it becomes zero at every place in a prescribed family. The resulting kernel records a local–global obstruction. To compare two such kernels we must transport the global class, the coefficient action, and all the local tests together. This comparison is used in the Sha branch of the model change in *PtConclOfIsoOf.lean:102–103*. Its proof takes place before any use of a global duality witness.

**Example 1 (degree one over a field).** [Expository] Work over $R=\mathbb F_5$. On each side take a complex concentrated in degrees zero and one, with $C^0=R$, $C^1=R^3$, and differential $b\mapsto(b,0,0)$. Thus every degree-one cochain is closed and each class has the unique representative $(0,y,z)$. Take two local complexes on each side, each with a single copy of $R$ in degree one. Write the sites as $a,b$ and $a',b'$ and match $a$ to $b'$ and $b$ to $a'$. The unprimed restrictions are $y,2y$ in that order. The primed restrictions are $z',z'$ in that order. These projections kill the boundaries and are chain maps.

Pull back globally by $(x',y',z')\mapsto(x',2z',3y')$ and locally by multiplication by $2$ at $a$ and $4$ at $b$. The global map fixes the degree-zero coordinate and carries the differential to itself. The two coordinates of restriction after global pullback are $2z',4z'$, exactly the two outputs of local pullback on the relabelled primed restrictions. Globally the inverse is $(x,y,z)\mapsto(x,2z,3y)$, since $2\cdot3=1$ in $R$. The local inverses multiply by $3$ and $4$ respectively with labels restored. The primed kernel consists of the five classes $(y',0)$ and the unprimed kernel of the five classes $(0,z)$. Pullback sends $(y',0)$ to $(0,3y')$, and its inverse sends $(0,z)$ to $(2z,0)$.

**Example 2 (degree two over a ring).** [Expository] Work over $R=\mathbb Z/4\mathbb Z$. On each side take $C^1=R$, $C^2=R^2$, and differential $d^1(t)=(2t,0)$, with other terms zero. The boundaries are $(0,0),(2,0)$. A degree-two class is $(\bar x,z)\in\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z\times R$, where the bar denotes reduction modulo $2$. Both local complexes have $\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z$ in degree two and zero elsewhere. Match $a$ with $b'$ and $b$ with $a'$. Use restrictions $(\bar x,\bar z)$ on the unprimed side and $(\bar z',\overline{x'+z'})$ on the primed side in the written orders. Every coordinate kills $(2,0)$, so the restrictions descend from chain maps.

Pull back globally by $(x',z')\mapsto(x'+z',3z')$ in degree two and the identity in degree one. The inverse in degree two is $(x,z)\mapsto(x-3z,3z)$, using $3^2=1$ in $R$. Both maps fix $(2t,0)$, and the local pullbacks are identities. The two restrictions after pullback are $(\overline{x'+z'},\bar z')$, which is the relabelled primed pair. The unprimed kernel has classes $(0,0),(0,2)$: both bars must vanish. On the primed side $\bar z'=0$ and $\overline{x'+z'}=0$ force $\bar x'=0$ and $z'\in\{0,2\}$. The pullback and its inverse both carry the two displayed classes to the classes with the same coordinates. At the representative level the nonzero primed kernel class $(0,2)$ goes to $(2,2)$, which differs from $(0,2)$ by a boundary. This quotient step is essential.

**Definition (the archive kernel).** Let $R$ be a discrete commutative ring, $N$ a discrete $R$-module with actions $\sigma$ and $\sigma'$ of topological groups $G$ and $G'$ respectively. Consider families of continuous homomorphisms $\phi_i:D_i\to G$ and $\phi'_j:D'_j\to G'$ indexed by sets $I,I'$. The groups on the local side act through these homomorphisms. Write $r_i^q$ and $r_j^{\prime q}$ for restriction on continuous cohomology in degree $q$. For $q=1,2$ the archive defines
$$K^q=\bigcap_{i\in I}\ker r_i^q,\qquad K^{\prime q}=\bigcap_{j\in I'}\ker r_j^{\prime q}.$$
The Lean declarations are *Sha1* and *Sha2* in *ContinuousConstSMulQuotient.lean:168–170*. This infimum of submodules is the kernel of the map $r^q:x\mapsto(r_i^q x)_i$ into the ordinary dependent product. Indeed, an element of that product is zero precisely when all its coordinates are zero. The concrete *loc1* and *loc2* use this product, as do *gloc1* and *gloc2*. For $I=\varnothing$ the product is zero and the kernel is the entire global group. [Source ledger](../sources/SOURCES.md#chapter-18-18317-transport-of-sha-kernels)

**Lemma 18.3.17A (the local tests commute with pullback).** [Lean, read] Fix a topological group isomorphism $u:G\to G'$, a bijection $s:I\to I'$, and topological group isomorphisms $v_i:D_i\to D'_{s(i)}$ for every $i$. Suppose $\phi'_{s(i)}v_i=u\phi_i$ for every $i$ and $\sigma'(u(g))m=\sigma(g)m$ for every $g,m$. For each $q=1,2$ pullback gives linear isomorphisms $A_q:H^q(G',N)\to H^q(G,N)$ and $B_{q,i}:H^q(D'_{s(i)},N)\to H^q(D_i,N)$ with
$$r_i^q A_q=B_{q,i}r_{s(i)}^{\prime q}.$$

**Proof.** On cochains pullback precomposes each argument and keeps $N$ fixed. The degree-one cocycle equation is $f(gh)=f(g)+g f(h)$ and a boundary is $g\mapsto gm-m$. Pullback carries the equation to the equation at $(u(g),u(h))$, since $u(gh)=u(g)u(h)$ and $u(g)$ acts as $g$. The pullback of the boundary of $m$ is the boundary of that same $m$. In degree two the equation is
$$g z(h,k)-z(gh,k)+z(g,hk)-z(g,h)=0.$$
After substitution the left side for the pulled-back cochain is exactly the primed left side at $(u(g),u(h),u(k))$. A degree-two boundary is
$$d c(g,h)=g c(h)-c(gh)+c(g)$$
for a continuous one-cochain $c$. Substituting $(u(g),u(h))$ shows that its pullback is the boundary of $c\circ u$. Continuity is preserved by composition with $u$ and $u\times u$. Addition and scalar multiplication are pointwise, so these constructions descend to linear maps on the quotients. The same argument with $u^{-1}$ applies because the action identity at $u^{-1}(g')$ is the inverse identity. Composing the two substitutions in either order recovers every cochain and hence every class. These are the two inverses constructed in *TransportA.lean:47–77*.

Locally, the action of $v_i(d)$ on the primed coefficient module is $\sigma'(\phi'_{s(i)}v_i(d))=\sigma'(u(\phi_i(d)))=\sigma(\phi_i(d))$. Thus the same construction applies to $v_i$. Restriction along $\phi_i$ is also precomposition, preserves the cocycle equations by multiplicativity and takes a boundary to the boundary of the restricted primitive by the displayed formulas. For a primed degree-one representative $f'$, the two paths at $d$ evaluate $f'$ at $u\phi_i(d)$ and at $\phi'_{s(i)}v_i(d)$. For degree two they evaluate $z'$ at the two tuples formed by these expressions at $d_1,d_2$. The arguments agree, so the representatives agree and their quotient classes agree. This is the quotient induction in *TateShafarevichTransport.lean:55–81*. $\square$

**Theorem 18.3.17B (both kernels, in both directions).** [Lean, read] Under the hypotheses of Lemma 18.3.17A, for every $q\in\{1,2\}$ and every $x'\in H^q(G',N)$,
$$x'\in K^{\prime q}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad A_q x'\in K^q.$$
The restrictions of $A_q$ and $A_q^{-1}$ give mutually inverse linear isomorphisms between $K^{\prime q}$ and $K^q$.

**Proof.** If all the primed restrictions vanish, the square gives $r_i^q A_q x'=B_{q,i}(0)=0$ for every $i$. Conversely, take any $j\in I'$. Put $i=s^{-1}(j)$. Vanishing of $r_i^q A_q x'$ gives $B_{q,i}r_j^{\prime q}x'=0$, so $r_j^{\prime q}x'=0$ by injectivity. This proves the equivalence. For any $y\in K^q$, put $x'=A_q^{-1}y$. The equivalence puts $x'$ in $K^{\prime q}$, so the inverse is defined on the whole kernel. Both composite identities are inherited from the ambient isomorphisms.

Equivalently, the ordinary product isomorphism is $(P_q y')_i=B_{q,i}y'_{s(i)}$, with $(P_q^{-1}y)_j=B_{q,s^{-1}(j)}^{-1}y_{s^{-1}(j)}$. In both composites the labels cancel and each local inverse cancels its pullback. Thus $r^q A_q=P_q r^{\prime q}$. In the code the two kernel membership proofs use the coordinate identity directly, followed by the proof that the image of the primed submodule is the unprimed one. The reverse image witness for $y$ is $A_q^{-1}y$. The resulting *nonempty_sha1_equiv* and *nonempty_sha2_equiv* package the respective linear equivalences in *Nonempty*. [Production proof, *TateShafarevichTransport.lean:13–22,83–119*](../sources/SOURCES.md#chapter-18-18317-transport-of-sha-kernels)

**Application (same number-field data).** Take any number fields $F,F'$ with algebraic closures $\Omega,\Omega'$ and an isomorphism $e:F\to F'$ lifted by $E:\Omega\to\Omega'$, meaning that the two restrictions of $E$ and $e$ agree on the embedded $F$. Use the inherited *LocHyp* and quotient comparison of §18.3.12. Fix a prime $\ell$, $r>0$, and the coefficient ring $R=\mathbb Z/\ell^r\mathbb Z$. Let $S$ be any finite set of finite primes containing those over $\ell$ and let $S'$ be its image under $e$. For every $v\in S$ choose a valuation ring $A_v$ above it, and put $A'_{s(v)}=E(A_v)$ with $s=e_I:S\to S'$. The global groups are $G=G_{F,S}$ and $G'=G_{F',S'}$, the quotients of the absolute groups by the closed normal subgroups generated by inertia outside the respective sets of finite primes. The map $u=\Psi$ comes from conjugation $\Phi$ by $E$. Locally $v_i=e_D(i)$ is the restriction of $\Phi$ to the decomposition groups of the chosen valuation rings. If $q_S$ and $q_{S'}$ denote the quotient maps, then for each $d\in D_i$,
$$\phi'_{s(i)}v_i(d)=q_{S'}(\Phi(d))=\Psi(q_S(d))=\Psi\phi_i(d).$$
Here the maps to the quotient are the actual *locFam* components. They need only be continuous homomorphisms. For any finite discrete $R$-module $M$ with continuous action $\rho$ of $G$, the action $\rho'=\rho\circ\Psi^{-1}$ on the same $M$ supplies the required identity for every $g,m$. This proves the claimed kernel transport for every choice in this setup. In the consumer the character also satisfies $\mu'=\mu\circ\Psi^{-1}$, with the factorization of the cyclotomic character supplied by the preceding comparison. [*Global.lean:128–156*, *GalCoh.lean:132–139* and *PtConclOfIsoOf.lean:22–61,82–89*](../sources/SOURCES.md#chapter-18-18317-transport-of-sha-kernels)

**Remarks and consumer contract.**

- **Which places are tested.** In *PtConclOfIsoOf* the phrase “every local restriction” quantifies over exactly $S$. There is no test outside $S$ in this definition. Unramifiedness outside $S$ belongs to the definition of the global quotient, and a separate condition requiring zero restriction there would strengthen the kernel definition. There is also no archimedean coordinate in *locFam*. For comparison with classical Poitou–Tate, adjoin the infinite places and use the local Tate cohomology convention there. An embedding $\tau:F\hookrightarrow\mathbb C$ goes to $\tau\circ e^{-1}$; this commutes with complex conjugation and preserves real type, and precomposition with $e$ is its inverse. Compatible local choices extend the coordinate argument to these places. In the consumer the guard $\ell\ne2$ or $F$ totally complex makes the added positive-degree tests zero. At a complex place the local group is trivial. At a real place with $2$ a unit in $R$, write $c$ for the nonidentity element of $C_2$. The cyclic resolution alternates $c-1$ and $1+c$. The projections $(1+c)/2$ and $(1-c)/2$ split the module into its two eigenspaces. The image of $c-1$ equals the kernel of $1+c$, and the image of $1+c$ equals the kernel of $c-1$. Thus both degree-one and degree-two groups vanish, also with Tate's convention. If $\ell=2$ and there is a real place, even the trivial $\mathbb F_2$-module gives $H^1(C_2,\mathbb F_2)=H^2(C_2,\mathbb F_2)=\mathbb F_2$ by the same resolution with zero differentials. An omitted real test then requires separate justification. [Milne, *Arithmetic Duality Theorems*, I §§2, 4](https://www.jmilne.org/math/Books/ADTnot.pdf)
- **Restricted products.** Classically, for an infinite family, the local tuples lie in distinguished unramified subgroups at all but finitely many places. In degrees one and two Milne's restricted products for finite coefficients use unramified degree-one classes and zero unramified degree-two classes at the good places. If a localization factors through the restricted product, its kernel is the same as for the full product because the inclusion is injective. To obtain an isomorphism of the restricted products themselves, suppose all but finitely many distinguished subgroups are carried bijectively onto their partners by the local isomorphisms. Then the coordinate formula and its inverse preserve the defining condition since the label bijection sends finite exceptional sets to finite sets. In the unramified case the subgroups are images of inflation from the residue quotients. A comparison carrying inertia onto inertia induces a residue-quotient isomorphism, and pulling back a residue cochain before or after inflation gives the same cochain by composition. Applying the inverse gives equality of the distinguished subgroups. For a finite family the “all but finitely many” condition is automatic. The Lean kernel statement uses the intersection itself and requires no restricted-product or inflation comparison. [Milne I §4, pp. 55–56, Lemma 4.8 and the definition preceding Theorem 4.10](https://www.jmilne.org/math/Books/ADTnot.pdf)
- **Failure under incompatible choices.** Return to Example 1 and change just the primed restriction at $b'$ to $y'$, keeping both local isomorphisms, the label bijection, and the global isomorphism. This is still a chain map. The primed kernel now requires $z'=y'=0$ while the unprimed kernel is still a line. The primed class $(1,0)$ maps to $(0,3)$ in the unprimed kernel but its restriction at $b'$ is $1$. At $a$ the two paths give $0$ and $2$. Thus even the abstract kernel cardinalities can differ after an incompatible local match. Permuting the entries of one fixed full product alone preserves its zero locus. It can still break the square: in Example 2 the identity labels on the primed class $(1,0)$ give local output $(0,1)$, while restricting its global pullback gives $(1,0)$. Here the failed match changes a tested restriction. Both examples are finite complexes modelling the cohomological interface, with no claimed realization by number fields or their decomposition groups.
- **The twisted coefficient and the two outputs.** Write $M^D=\operatorname{Hom}_R(M,R)$ with action $(g\lambda)(m)=\mu(g)\lambda(\rho(g^{-1})m)$ and use the corresponding primed action. Because $\Psi(g^{-1})=\Psi(g)^{-1}$, the identities for $\rho$ and $\mu$ make these actions agree under pullback. This proves the hypothesis for *twDual_compat_of_iso*. Apply Theorem 18.3.17B to $M$ for $a_1,a_2$ and to $M^D$ for $b_1,b_2$, all from primed to unprimed and all along the same group and label maps. The consumer uses $(b_1,a_2)$ on $(\operatorname{Sha}^1(M^D),\operatorname{Sha}^2(M))$ and $(a_1,b_2)$ on $(\operatorname{Sha}^1(M),\operatorname{Sha}^2(M^D))$. Here $\operatorname{Sha}$ denotes the $K$ defined above. [*GalCohDefs.lean:29–68* and *TateShafarevichTransport.lean:123–151*](../sources/SOURCES.md#chapter-18-18317-transport-of-sha-kernels)
- **What the consumer can conclude.** Each pair in *ShaG* requires finiteness of both modules, existence of a linear isomorphism from the degree-two module to the $R$-linear dual of the degree-one module, and equality of their cardinalities. Given these on the primed side, denote the two kernel equivalences for that pair by $a:A'\to A$ and $b:B'\to B$ and the supplied witness by $f':B'\to\operatorname{Hom}_R(A',R)$. The transported witness is $f(y)=f'(b^{-1}y)\circ a^{-1}$, with inverse $\lambda\mapsto b((f')^{-1}(\lambda\circ a))$. Substitution cancels both equivalences in each composite. The two bijections transport finiteness and give $|A|=|A'|=|B'|=|B|$. The proof *dualData_transfer* implements these three steps. The *shaDual_iff* conversions in *PtConclOfIsoOf.lean:102–103* are definitional: unpack the primed *ShaDual* as *ShaG*, apply *tate_shafarevich_transport*, and repack the unprimed *ShaG*. It is the second component of *PTConclAt* at line 80 that supplies the primed witness. [*TateShafarevichTransport.lean:24–33,145–151* and *NineTermExactIff.lean:55–63,79–80*](../sources/SOURCES.md#chapter-18-18317-transport-of-sha-kernels)
- **Global duality boundary.** The output transports existence of the two abstract duality witnesses. It makes no assertion identifying a specific canonical global pairing. The classical existence and finiteness input is the duality theorem of Tate, with parts obtained independently by Poitou, as described by Milne. The named single-field *PtfsHyp* input, production of normalized invariants, and the six local/trace comparison bodies for the separate nine-term branch retain their existing reading status. The local R3 designation records comparison with the indicated source bodies. Neither the examples nor the source reading runs the Lean kernel or checks the RH argument. [Milne I §4, Theorem 4.10(a) and Notes, pp. 56–57 and 66](https://www.jmilne.org/math/Books/ADTnot.pdf)
- **Classical hypotheses.** Tate's theorem concerns a finite continuous module for the Galois group unramified outside the indexing set, which includes the infinite places and all primes where the module order is not a unit. For a finite module killed by $n=\ell^r$ the latter primes are among the primes over $\ell$. In this setting the character module of the classical theorem is $\operatorname{Hom}(M,\mu_n)$, since images of homomorphisms from $M$ have exponent dividing $n$. The chosen primitive root identifies $R$ additively with $\mu_n$ by $a\mapsto\zeta^a$, giving exactly the character action used above. A pairing valued in $\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z$ on these $R$-modules takes values in the $n$-torsion subgroup, identified with $R$ by $a\mapsto a/n+\mathbb Z$. Additive maps between modules killed by $n$ are $R$-linear. These identifications explain the duality contract. The call at line 80 still supplies the actual witness for this proof. [Tate, Stockholm ICM, §3, Theorem 3.1(a), pp. 290–291](https://www.mathunion.org/fileadmin/ICM/Proceedings/ICM1962.1/ICM1962.1.ocr.pdf)

**Review.** Same-source preliminary acceptance covers the kernel transport and the two transported duality witnesses under the stated hypotheses. [§18.3.19](#18319-the-arithmetic-sha-dictionary-and-its-restriction-squares) compares the two presentations of the localization kernels used over a single model field.

### 18.3.18 Single-model assembly

**[Open]** The single-model assembly is reserved here pending review. Continue with [§18.3.19](#18319-the-arithmetic-sha-dictionary-and-its-restriction-squares), which states the arithmetic inputs it uses and proves the comparison of localization kernels.

### 18.3.19 The arithmetic Sha dictionary and its restriction squares

**Motivation.** The transport in [§18.3.17](#18317-transporting-the-tateshafarevich-kernels-in-degrees-one-and-two) compares kernels across fields. Over a single model field the same kernels are presented using continuous cocycles on a quotient of the absolute Galois group, with local tests on decomposition groups. The arithmetic pairing is supplied in a presentation using a Galois group of a maximal unramified-outside-$S$ extension and absolute Galois groups of completions. To apply that pairing to the original kernels, one needs to identify the two localization maps on classes. Even isomorphic global and local groups can give distinct localization maps if the comparison fails to identify their arrows.

**Example [Expository].** Let $G=C_4$, let its generator act by $-1$ on $\mathbb Z/4\mathbb Z$, and use the order-two subgroup as the local group. A crossed homomorphism taking the generator to $1$ vanishes on its square, even though the value $1$ is impossible for a global coboundary at the generator. Thus a nonzero global class survives the local kernel test. The full calculation below also exhibits a degree-two class whose local *cocycle* is nonzero but whose local *class* is zero.

**Definition (the two arithmetic presentations).** Fix a number field $K$ embedded in $\Omega=\overline{\mathbb Q}$ with the algebraic closure structure of the archive, a finite set $S$ of finite places, and, for each $v\in S$, a valuation subring $A_v$ of $\Omega$ lying over $v$. Denote by $N_S$ the closed normal subgroup of $G_K=\operatorname{Gal}(\Omega/K)$ generated by the inertia groups outside $S$. Write $G=G_K/N_S$, $L_v=\operatorname{Dec}(A_v)$ and $\lambda_v:L_v\to G$ for inclusion followed by quotient. Let $F_S$ denote the maximal subextension of $\Omega/K$ unramified outside $S$. The second global group is $H=\operatorname{Gal}(F_S/K)$. Its local group at $v$ is $H_v=\operatorname{Gal}(\overline{K_v}/K_v)$, with $K_v$ the adic completion. An embedding $\iota_v:\Omega\hookrightarrow\overline{K_v}$ over $K$ determines the continuous restriction $\phi_v:H_v\to H$ by
$$
\iota_v(\phi_v(\sigma)y)=\sigma(\iota_v(y))\qquad(y\in F_S).
$$
The displayed equality uses the inclusion $F_S\subset\Omega$. These local-to-global maps are continuous homomorphisms and may have kernels.

**Lemma (the group square) [Lean, read].** For every such $K,S,(A_v)$, there are compatible choices of $\iota_v$, a topological group isomorphism $\theta:G\to H$, and group isomorphisms $b_v:H_v\to L_v$ continuous in both directions, satisfying $\theta\lambda_v b_v=\phi_v$ for all $v$.

**Proof.** Restriction to $F_S$ from $G_K$ is continuous for the Krull topologies and surjective by extension of automorphisms in a Galois extension. The fixing-subgroup identity `NS_eq_fixingSubgroup` identifies its kernel with $N_S$. Thus $\theta$ is induced by restriction, with $\theta([\tau])y=\tau y$. Its continuity is the quotient universal property. The quotient of the compact absolute Galois group is compact, and $H$ is Hausdorff, so the continuous bijection has continuous inverse. This is the construction in `PtfsGsEquiv.lean:21–51`.

For each lifted place, choose a local embedding compatible with $A_v$ and the decomposition-to-completion isomorphism for that embedding, using `exists_isLocEmb` and `decomposition_mulEquiv_galLoc`. Their wrapper `exists_algHom_mulEquiv_dec` takes the inverse of the latter isomorphism, swaps its two continuity witnesses, and obtains
$$
\iota_v(b_v(\sigma)x)=\sigma(\iota_v(x))\qquad(x\in\Omega).
$$
For $x=y\in F_S$, the left side is $\iota_v(\theta\lambda_v b_v(\sigma)y)$ and the right side is $\iota_v(\phi_v(\sigma)y)$. Injectivity of the **same** $\iota_v$ gives equality on each $y$, hence equality of the automorphisms. `localCmp'` chooses the witnesses together. Its output embedding family is indexed by the subtype of membership in the set underlying $S$, whereas the input decomposition family uses membership in the finite set. The relabeling sends a pair consisting of a prime and its finite-set membership proof to the same prime with the corresponding set membership proof, and vice versa. Hence the embedding used on either leg is the same selected embedding. $\square$

**Definition (cochains and kernels).** Let $R$ be a commutative ring, $M$ a discrete $R$-module with continuous $G$-action $\rho$, and $A$ a discrete representation of $H$ over the lifted integers, with underlying additive group $V$. Discreteness on the second side is expressed by open stabilizers, so the action on $V$ with its discrete topology is continuous. Indeed, for fixed $a$ each fiber of its orbit map is empty or a coset of an open stabilizer. Together with the open singleton $\{a\}$ this gives joint continuity. Suppose $u:M\to V$ is an additive isomorphism satisfying $u(gm)=\theta(g)u(m)$. At a local group the respective actions are via $\lambda_v$ and $\phi_v$.

Use all continuous inhomogeneous cochains. A degree-one cochain $f$ is a cocycle when $f(gh)=f(g)+g f(h)$, and a degree-two cochain $z$ is a cocycle when
$$
g z(h,k)-z(gh,k)+z(g,hk)-z(g,h)=0.
$$
Boundaries in degrees one and two are, respectively, $d^0m(g)=gm-m$ and $d^1c(g,h)=g c(h)-c(gh)+c(g)$ for a continuous $c$. Cohomology is the quotient of cocycles by these boundaries. The categorical complex on the second side presents its cochains as locally constant functions on finite tuples. A function to a discrete group is continuous exactly when it is locally constant.

**Proposition 18.3.19 (restriction-compatible comparison) [Lean, read].** For any topological groups and coefficient data of the indicated types, any index family, and any continuous $\lambda_v,\phi_v$ with topological group isomorphisms $\theta,b_v$ satisfying $\theta\lambda_v b_v=\phi_v$, there are additive isomorphisms, for each $q\in\{1,2\}$,
$$
E_q:H^q(G,M)\longrightarrow H^q(H,V),\qquad
E_{q,v}:H^q(L_v,M)\longrightarrow H^q(H_v,V).
$$
Here the groups on the right use the categorical continuous cohomology presentation. For every $v$ and every class $x$, $E_{q,v}\operatorname{res}_{\lambda_v}x=\operatorname{res}_{\phi_v}E_qx$. For the group data of the lemma, these give the two additive equivalences of `shaIso_of_rep` between $\mathrm{Sha}^q$ and $\mathrm{ptSha}$.

**Proof.** First consider any continuous group homomorphism $a:B\to C$ and additive map $w$ from a $C$-module to a $B$-module intertwining the action via $a$. The coefficient targets here are discrete. Define $Tf(t)=w(f(at))$ and $Tz(t,s)=w(z(at,as))$, and take $Tm=w(m)$ on zero-cochains. Write $(df)$ for $d^1 f$ and use $d^2z$ for the four-term expression above. Then
$$
\begin{aligned}
d^0(Tm)(t)&=w((at)m-m),\\
d^1(Tf)(t,s)&=w((at)f(as)-f((at)(as))+f(at)),\\
d^2(Tz)(t,s,r)&=w((at)z(as,ar)-z((at)(as),ar)+z(at,(as)(ar))-z(at,as)).
\end{aligned}
$$
Each is the corresponding transported differential. Thus both cocycle equations and both boundary relations are preserved. This also identifies the actual boundary witnesses: $m$ becomes $w(m)$ and $c$ becomes $w\circ c\circ a$. All tuple substitutions are continuous, and every coefficient map between discrete groups is continuous. The product of two copies of $a$ is used in degree two, and the triple product appears in the cocycle equation.

Globally take $(a,w)=(\theta^{-1},u)$, and at $v$ take $(b_v,u)$. Locally the coefficient identity is $u((\lambda_v b_v(\sigma))m)=\phi_v(\sigma)u(m)$. For the inverses take $(\theta,u^{-1})$ and $(b_v^{-1},u^{-1})$ respectively. These satisfy the reverse action identities by substituting along the original isomorphisms and applying $u^{-1}$. The cochain formulas compose to identity in both orders, including on boundaries. At the group-pullback stage in Lean, injectivity is proved by reflecting a boundary along the inverse group map. In degree one this uses the same $m$ and evaluates at the inverse image of the argument. In degree two it pulls the continuous primitive $c$ back along the inverse. Surjectivity uses the cocycle precomposed with the inverse, on one or both arguments. The identity $a a^{-1}=1$ gives the original action on the reconstructed cocycle, and $a^{-1}a=1$ recovers the representative after restriction.

The second stage uses `gc1` or `gc2` on the now common group. For every source cocycle there is a categorical cocycle with values $u(f(s_0))$ or $u(z(s_0,s_1))$, where $s$ is a tuple indexed by $\operatorname{Fin}(q)$. These functions are locally constant and closed by the differential identities. Conversely, evaluate a categorical cocycle on the constant one-tuple or the ordered two-tuple and apply $u^{-1}$ to its values. Every tuple of length one or two is recovered by these coordinates, so this operation gives a continuous closed representative and inverts the value comparison. The zero-class criterion is existence of a locally constant primitive, transported back by $u^{-1}$ with the same coordinate rule. In degree one its domain is a point and in degree two its domain is the group. Thus the quotient comparison `core_quot` gives precisely the class of any categorical representative with the required values. The archive chooses local equivalences with this representative rule simultaneously for the whole family. It uses `LinearEquiv.ofBijective` for the group-pullback stage and additive equivalences for the composites.

For degree one, on $f$ the two routes to $H_v$ have values
$$
u(f(\lambda_v b_v(\sigma)))=u(f(\theta^{-1}\phi_v(\sigma))).
$$
For degree two their values on $(\sigma,\tau)$ are
$$
u(z(\lambda_v b_v(\sigma),\lambda_v b_v(\tau)))
=u(z(\theta^{-1}\phi_v(\sigma),\theta^{-1}\phi_v(\tau))).
$$
Each argument agrees by the group square. On the categorical side `ptRes` uses the identity coefficient map. The equality between restriction of the class and the class of the restricted cocycle is `cohomologyπ_cohomologyMapAlong`, with its value formula `cochainOf_cocycleι_cocyclesMapAlong`. Hence these pointwise equalities prove the two identities on quotients. This is the final representative argument of `resCmp1'` and `resCmp2B`.

In degree one, membership in `Sha1` means that all `resH1` values vanish, by the membership rule for the infimum of the linear kernels. Membership in `ptSha` in degree one is vanishing of the product map, hence vanishing of every `ptRes` value at the corresponding set label. The degree-one square and injectivity of $E_{1,v}$ identify these tests at every place. In degree two use the separate infimum for `Sha2`, the degree-two `ptSha` product kernel, and injectivity of $E_{2,v}$ in the degree-two square. In each case the membership equivalence is $x\in\mathrm{Sha}^q\iff E_qx\in\mathrm{ptSha}^q$. The forward map on the subtypes is $x\mapsto E_qx$. For an element $y$ in the product kernel, take $E_q^{-1}y$ and apply the reverse implication to show it belongs to the infimum. The two composites reduce to the ambient inverse identities. This is the kernel restriction of `addEquiv_of_resCmp` used twice by `shaIso_of_rep`. The argument also covers an empty local family, where both tests are vacuous. $\square$

**First hand calculation [Expository].** Use the nonsplit sequence $1\to C_2\to C_4\to C_2\to1$, writing elements of $C_4$ as $i=0,1,2,3$ and its local subgroup as $\{0,2\}$. The sequence is nonsplit because both lifts of the nonidentity quotient element have order four. Use $M=\mathbb Z/4\mathbb Z$ with action $i m=(-1)^i m$. On the second copy take $\theta(i)=-i$, the identity map $b_v$ of the order-two groups, and $u(m)=-m$. Give the second copy the same sign action and use the same local inclusion. Both group and action squares hold.

In degree one every cocycle is $f_a=(0,a,0,a)$, where $a=f_a(1)$ ranges over $M$. The crossed-homomorphism equation follows from $\epsilon(i+j)=\epsilon(i)+(-1)^i\epsilon(j)$ with $\epsilon(i)$ the parity in $\{0,1\}$. A boundary has $a=-2m$, so there are exactly two classes represented by $a=0,1$. Their restrictions are zero as cochains. The comparison sends $f_a$ to $f_{-a}$. The local action is trivial, with exactly two local cocycles given by values $0,2$ at $2$ and zero local boundaries. The local comparison negates those values, leaving both unchanged.

In degree two any cocycle can be normalized by subtracting $d^1c$, with $c$ constant equal to $z(0,0)$. The cocycle equation gives $z(0,j)=z(0,0)$ and $z(i,0)=i\cdot z(0,0)$, which are exactly the corresponding boundary values. For a normalized cocycle set $c(0)=c(1)=0$, $c(2)=-z(1,1)$, $c(3)=-c(2)-z(1,2)$. Subtracting $d^1c$ leaves row $1$ zero except possibly its last entry $t$ at column $3$. The equation on $(1,i,j)$ recursively determines row $i+1$ from row $i$ and row $1$. It gives the carry function
$$
z_t(i,j)=t\left\lfloor\frac{i+j}{4}\right\rfloor,
\qquad t\in\{0,2\}.
$$
Indeed, the recursion with $j=1,2,3$ gives row $2$ equal to $(0,0,t,-t)$ and row $3$ equal to $(0,t,-t,t)$, and at $(1,3,1)$ closure gives $2t=0$. Conversely, the two sums of carries for the two parenthesizations of $i+j+k$ equal $\lfloor(i+j+k)/4\rfloor$, and $t$ is fixed by the action, so these are cocycles. To distinguish the two classes, examine the fourth power of a lift of the generator in the associated extension. Explicitly use multiplication
$$
(m,i)(n,j)=(m+(-1)^i n+z(i,j),i+j\bmod 4).
$$
Associativity is the cocycle equation, and $(0,1)^4=(t,0)$ for $z_t$. Replacing the lift adds the norm $(1-1+1-1)m=0$. For a boundary $z=d^1c$ the coordinate change $(m,i)\mapsto(m+c(i),i)$ identifies the group with the split extension, so $t=0$. Thus the two carry representatives give both classes.

On the local subgroup the carry has only one possibly nonzero value, $z_t(2,2)=t$. With the trivial local action this is $d^1c_v$ for $c_v(0)=0,c_v(2)=t/2$ using the integer representatives $0,2$. A normalized local two-cocycle has arbitrary value at $(2,2)$ and boundaries change it by even values, so local degree two has two classes and the restriction of both global classes is zero. The global comparison gives $-z_t(-i,-j)$. For $i,j>0$, its carry is $1$ precisely when $i+j\le4$, while the original carry is $1$ when $i+j\ge4$. Their difference modulo four is the boundary of $c'(0)=0,c'(i)=t$ for $i>0$. This follows since $t$ is invariant and $2t=0$, so $d^1c'$ is $t$ precisely when both inputs are nonzero and their sum is nonzero modulo four. The local comparison negates the primitive, giving the identical restricted cochain. The two kernels in this example have order two.

**Second hand calculation [Expository].** Let $G=C_4\times C_2$, with elements $(i,j)$ in least nonnegative coordinates, and use the trivial module $\mathbb F_2$. This is a nonsplit extension of $C_2\times C_2$ by the first factor's subgroup $\langle(2,0)\rangle$, since $(1,0)$ in the quotient has only order-four lifts. Use local tests $\lambda_a(1)=(2,0)$ and $\lambda_b(1)=(0,1)$ from two copies of $C_2$. In the second presentation put $\theta(i,j)=(i,j+i\bmod 2)$, the identity coefficient map $u$, and use the same two embeddings at the corresponding labels, which can be listed in reverse order. Both embedded elements are fixed by $\theta$, and both $b_v$ are identity maps.

All degree-one representatives are $f_{p,q}(i,j)=pi+qj$, with $p,q\in\mathbb F_2$, and boundaries vanish. Restriction takes $(p,q)$ to $(0,q)$ in the order $a,b$. The global comparison sends $(p,q)$ to $(p+q,q)$, agreeing with both local maps. The kernel is the two classes with $q=0$.

In degree two, for parameters $P,Q,U\in\mathbb F_2$ take the representatives
$$
z_{P,Q,U}((i,j),(k,l))
=P\left\lfloor\frac{i+k}{4}\right\rfloor+Qjl+Ujk.
$$
The carry identity proves the first summand closed, and the other two summands are products of additive parity characters, whose four-term differential cancels. These are all the classes. To see this, normalize an arbitrary cocycle as above and form its extension by the multiplication formula with trivial action. For lifts $x,y$ of $(1,0),(0,1)$, the elements $x^4=P$, $y^2=Q$, and $yx(xy)^{-1}=U$ lie in the central copy of $\mathbb F_2$. Changing a lift by an element of that copy changes none of the three values. The section $x^iy^j$ yields exactly the displayed cocycle, by commuting $y^j$ past $x^k$ and reducing powers. Every other normalized section differs by a one-cochain, whose change to the cocycle is its boundary. Thus all eight triples occur and are distinct. Locally a normalized two-cocycle is determined by its value on the nonzero pair, and a normalized coboundary has zero there. The two local classes are therefore $(P,Q)$, and the degree-two kernel is the two triples with $P=Q=0$.

The pullback by $\theta^{-1}=\theta$ uses the displayed cocycle at $((i,j+i),(k,l+k))$ with second coordinates reduced modulo two. On each local subgroup the input pair is unchanged, so the two restricted representatives agree exactly. Expanding the pullback replaces the last two terms by $Qjl+(Q+U)jk+Qil+(Q+U)ik$. Over $\mathbb F_2$, the term $il+jk$ is the boundary of $(i,j)\mapsto ij$, and $ik$ is the boundary of $(i,j)\mapsto\lfloor i/2\rfloor\bmod 2$, by the carry into the high binary digit. This gives the class parameters $(P,Q,U)$ again. In particular the pullback of the kernel generator $jk$ differs from it by the explicit nonzero boundary $ik$.

**Failure when the square is omitted.** In the second calculation keep both ambient comparisons, but replace only $\phi_b(1)$ by $(2,0)$. The local groups, topology, coefficient actions, and all comparison isomorphisms still exist. The degree-one class $j$ now restricts to zero after global comparison at both target labels, although its source restriction at $b$ is $1$. For the degree-two class $jl$, the pullback has value $1$ on the original pair of generators at $b$ and $0$ on the new pair, while its source restriction at $b$ represents the nonzero local class. With the modified family the target kernels have orders four in both degrees, whereas the source kernels have orders two. Thus deleting the square even permits failure of abstract kernel isomorphism. Both finite calculations illustrate cochain algebra [Expository].

**Remark (from the dictionary to the arithmetic consumer) [Lean, read].** Fix an intermediate number field $K\subset\overline{\mathbb Q}$ with the archive's algebraic closure structure, a prime $\ell$ and $r>0$ with $\ell\ne2$ or $K$ totally complex. Choose a primitive $\ell^r$th root $\zeta$, a normalized invariant family, a finite set $S$ containing every finite place above $\ell$ and a lift $A_v$ above every $v\in S$. Supply a character $\mu$ on the quotient whose pullback is the cyclotomic character modulo $\ell^r$, and a finite discrete coefficient module $M$ with continuous representation $\rho$ of the quotient. These are the inputs of `shaDictFS`. Put $N=\ell^r$, $R=\mathbb Z/N\mathbb Z$, and let $D$ denote the roots-of-unity representation `gsRoots S N` of $H$. The wrapper `repCmp` regards $M$ as an integral representation, then pulls its action across $\theta^{-1}$. Write $A$ for the resulting representation and $A^D=\operatorname{Hom}(A,D)$ with action $(h f)(a)=h f(h^{-1}a)$.

The root $\zeta$ lies in $F_S$ by the roots-of-unity supply, since every place dividing $N$ belongs to $S$. Its powers give the additive equivalence $R\simeq D$, with multiplication of roots written additively in $D$. Restriction of global automorphisms to $F_S$ and the stipulated cyclotomic factorization make this equivalence intertwine the twist $\mu$ with the action on $D$. If $t:R\to D$ denotes this equivalence, `dualCmp'` sends a twisted linear functional $f$ to $t\circ f\circ u^{-1}$, with inverse $F\mapsto t^{-1}\circ F\circ u$. Additive maps between $N$-torsion $R$-modules are $R$-linear by integer multiplication. Applying the action formula to the argument $u(m)$ verifies equivariance in both coordinates. The bidual map $A\to (A^D)^D$ is evaluation. It is equivariant because $(h\,\mathrm{ev}_a)(f)=h((h^{-1}f)(a))=f(ha)$. For bijectivity the archive uses `bijective_toDualDual` with $D\simeq R$, finiteness, and $N$-torsion. The comparison $M\to(A^D)^D$ is $u$ followed by evaluation.

The following are the actual witnesses in `PtfsShaFinal.lean`. All four use the same field, place family, embeddings and exponent.

| Dictionary package | Degree-one equivalence | Degree-two equivalence |
| --- | --- | --- |

The first package deliberately uses the bidual in degree two because the supplied pairing couples degree one of a coefficient with degree two of its dual. The two packages are formed on lines 70–75. Primality gives $N\ne0$, prime-ideal membership of $\ell^r$ implies membership of $\ell$, and the prime guard gives $N$ odd or $K$ totally complex. Finiteness and $N$-torsion of $A$ come from $M$, and those of $A^D$ from its finite domain and finite $N$-torsion codomain. The archive supplies the discrete action on each dual separately. For the source twist, continuity of $\mu$ follows from continuity of its pullback and the quotient topology. At a fixed functional the fiber of its orbit map is an intersection of open evaluation fibers indexed by the finite set $M$. This gives continuity on the discrete twisted dual. The normalized invariant family is quantified in the interface, but its value and normalization proof do not enter this dictionary construction.

Line 77 is `shaCmpFS' := shaCmpFS_of_dict shaDictFS`. The consumer specializes the dictionary at the original tuple and invokes `perfectQZ_of_found` twice. That lemma takes the finite perfect pairing of `poitouTate_sha_gs`, flips its order to degree two then degree one, and pulls it back by the two additive equivalences. Since both original groups are $N$-torsion, any pairing value in $\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z$ belongs to its $N$-torsion, identified with $R$ by $a\mapsto a/N\bmod\mathbb Z$. The inverse character correspondence precomposes with the inverse kernel equivalence and identifies values using the inverse of this torsion isomorphism. Every additive character between these $R$-modules respects scalar multiplication because scalars are integer residues. The pullback and value conversion therefore give both adjoints bijectively and yield the two $R$-linear perfect pairings required by `ShaCmpFS`. `PoitouTateNormalisedOfNine.lean:22` consumes `shaCmpFS'`. This witnesses the arithmetic input domain. The computed kernel orders refer to the finite group examples.

**Literature and reading boundary.** [Paper] Tate's ICM Theorem 3.1(a) states perfect finite duality for the degree-two localization kernel and the degree-one kernel of the dual, and credits independent work by Poitou. Milne gives the order used by the foundation pairing in Theorem I.4.10(a), using continuous cochains for discrete modules (I, p. 2). Apply that statement to either finite coefficient above with the archimedean places adjoined to $S$. Finiteness and $N$-torsion imply that the order of the coefficient has prime factors among those of $N$, which are inverted in the $S$-integers. The $N$-torsion dual is the homomorphism group into the $N$th roots of unity. At complex places the positive-degree kernels receive no test, and at real places the positive-degree groups vanish when $N$ is odd by averaging over the order-two group. The finite restricted product reduces to the product at the stated places. These facts identify the classical input of the consumer.
