### 18.3.23 Open subgroups, larger fixed fields, and the local family

**Motivation.** In §18.3.22 the pairing is computed over a number field $L$. Its consumer starts with an open subgroup $U$ of $G_{K,S}$, where $K$ and the set $S$ of finite places were fixed before $U$ was chosen. Which $L$ gives precisely $U$ inside that original group? Which local terms make the upper-field cup product a cup product for $U$? Fixing more field elements gives a smaller subgroup. At a place, one original local map can yield several maps after passage to $U$.

**Example 1 (a nonnormal cubic).** Let $K=\mathbb Q$, $\alpha=\sqrt[3]{2}$, and $E=\mathbb Q(\alpha,\zeta_3)$. The polynomial $X^3-2$ is Eisenstein at $2$. Its real cubic field acquires both other roots on adjoining the nonreal quadratic root $\zeta_3$. Thus its splitting field $E$ has degree $6$. Number the roots $1,2,3$ in the order $\alpha,\zeta_3\alpha,\zeta_3^2\alpha$. The action on them identifies $\Gamma=\operatorname{Gal}(E/\mathbb Q)$ with $S_3$. The subgroup $B=\{1,(23)\}$ fixes $L=\mathbb Q(\alpha)$. Take $S=\{2,3,5,7\}$. The polynomial discriminant $-108$ implies that the splitting field is unramified outside $2,3$, so $E$ lies in $K_S$. The inverse image $U$ of $B$ in $G_{K,S}$ has index $3$ and fixes exactly $L$.

1. Modulo $5$, $X^3-2=(X-3)(X^2+3X+4)$. The quadratic discriminant is $3$ modulo $5$, a nonsquare. Choose the prime of $E$ with decomposition group $D=\{1,(23)\}$. The complete list of double cosets is

   $$
   B1D=\{1,(23)\},\qquad B(12)D=\{(12),(123),(132),(13)\}.
   $$

   Compose permutations from right to left. The two $B$-orbits on the primes of $E$, written as $\Gamma/D$, are $\{D\}$ and $\{(12)D,(13)D\}$. Their stabilizers at the displayed representatives are $B$ and $\{1\}$. The $D$-orbits on the embeddings of $L$ have labels $\{1\}$ and $\{2,3\}$, where the representative $x$ corresponds to $x^{-1}(1)$. The two local degrees are $1,2$, the indices of $D\cap x^{-1}Bx$ in $D$. In particular the degree-one completion of $L$ still has a prime of $E$ above it with decomposition group $B$ over $L$.
2. The cubes modulo $7$ are $0,1,-1$, so the cubic is irreducible there. Here $D=\{1,(123),(132)\}$. The only double coset is $B1D=\Gamma$. The $B$-orbit on $\Gamma/D$ is $\{D,(23)D\}$ with trivial stabilizer, and $D$ has the single orbit $\{1,2,3\}$ on embeddings. There is one upper place of local degree $3$.
3. At $2$ the cubic is Eisenstein, and at $3$ the shifted polynomial $(X-1)^3-2=X^3-3X^2+3X-3$ is Eisenstein. The polynomial $X^2+X+1$ is irreducible modulo $2$, and its shift $X^2+3X+3$ is Eisenstein at $3$. At either prime the local splitting field contains extensions of degrees $3$ and $2$. Its degree is divisible by $6$ and at most $6$, so $D=\Gamma$. The only double coset is $B1\Gamma=\Gamma$, the prime orbit is $\{D\}$ with stabilizer $B$, and $D$ is transitive on embeddings. Each prime contributes one upper place of degree $3$. Thus $S_L$ has five places. Conjugation by $(12)$ takes $B$ to $\{1,(13)\}$. The three cosets count embeddings of $L$, whose group of $\mathbb Q$-automorphisms is trivial because only one root of the cubic lies in $L\subset\mathbb R$.

**Example 2 (a cyclic number field).** Take $K=\mathbb Q$, $L=E=\mathbb Q(\zeta_3)$, $\Gamma=\{1,c\}$ with $c$ complex conjugation, and $S=\{2,3,7\}$. Here $B=1$ and $U$ is the kernel of $G_{K,S}\to\Gamma$. This cyclotomic field is ramified only at $3$.

1. Modulo $7$, $X^2+X+1=(X-2)(X-4)$ with simple roots. Each root lifts to $\mathbb Q_7$. Here $D=1$, so the double cosets $\{1\},\{c\}$ give two singleton orbits on primes with trivial stabilizers. There are two copies of $G_{\mathbb Q_7}$ in the local family, indexed by different places.
2. Modulo $2$ the polynomial has no root. There is one double coset $\{1,c\}$ because $2$ is inert and $D=\Gamma$. The prime orbit is a singleton with trivial $B$-stabilizer. The local source is the index-two subgroup of $G_{\mathbb Q_2}$ fixing the unramified quadratic completion. At $3$ the shifted Eisenstein polynomial gives a single ramified upper place, again with local degree $2$ and one double coset $\{1,c\}$. Its local source fixes the ramified quadratic completion. Thus $S_L$ has four places.
3. Take $n=3$ and $W_0=U$. Then $W_0$ fixes $\mu_3$, both families cover the primes over $\ell=3$, and $n$ is odd. The roots coefficient upstairs is the additive form of $\mu_3\subset L$, and downstairs it is the same group in $K_S$ with action restricted to $U$. Both actions are trivial. An ordinary degree-one input is the character of $\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb Q(\zeta_9)/L)$ sending the exponent $4$ to $1$ in $\mathbb Z/3\mathbb Z$. This is a quotient of $G_{L,S_L}$ because the cyclotomic layer is unramified outside $3$. At either prime over $7$, Frobenius has exponent $7=4^2\bmod 9$, so the restriction has value $2$ on Frobenius. For a compact input of degree $2$, each of the two local cochain coordinates over $7$ is in degree $1$. Each cups with the corresponding local restriction of the ordinary input, producing a degree-two roots cochain in the local part of the compact degree-three output.

**Definitions.** Let $K$ be a finite subfield of $\Omega=\overline{\mathbb Q}$, $S$ a finite set of finite places of $K$, and $K_S\subseteq\Omega$ the maximal extension unramified outside $S$ at finite places. Each conjugate of a finite extension unramified outside $S$ is unramified outside $S$, because conjugation permutes places over each base place and preserves ramification indices. Taking composita gives a Galois extension $K_S/K$. Write $G=\operatorname{Gal}(K_S/K)$ with its Krull topology. Fix $K$-embeddings $\iota_v:\Omega\hookrightarrow\overline{K_v}$ for $v\in S$. Let $H_v=\operatorname{Gal}(\overline{K_v}/K_v)$ and let $\lambda_v:H_v\to G$ be restriction through $\iota_v$ to $K_S$. Its image $D_v$ is the decomposition group at the induced place of $K_S$. The map can have a kernel. For an intermediate field $L$, write

$$
 S_L=\operatorname{placesOver}(S)=\{w:\mathfrak p_w\cap\mathcal O_K=\mathfrak p_v\text{ for some }v\in S\}.
$$

The Mackey index for $U\leq G$ consists of $(v,UxD_v)$. For a representative $x$, its local source and map to $U$ are

$$
 H_{v,x}=\{\sigma\in H_v:x\lambda_v(\sigma)x^{-1}\in U\},\qquad
 \lambda_{v,x}(\sigma)=x\lambda_v(\sigma)x^{-1}.
$$

Thus $H_{v,x}$ is the preimage of $x^{-1}Ux\cap D_v$ in $H_v$. The examples compute the finite images that specify these preimages.

**Theorem 18.3.23a (the finite fixed field) [Lean, read].** Every open subgroup $U$ of $G$ is the pointwise fixing subgroup of a unique finite intermediate field $K\subseteq L\subseteq K_S$. With the inclusion into $\Omega$ this subgroup is precisely the archive's `gsOpenSubgroup` for $L\hookrightarrow K_S$. Also $[L:K]=[G:U]$, and $U_1\subseteq U_2$ gives $K_S^{U_2}\subseteq K_S^{U_1}$. The identification $G/U\simeq\operatorname{Gal}(L/K)$ as groups requires $U$ to be normal.

**Proof.** An open subgroup is closed because the other cosets form an open complement. The Krull neighbourhood basis gives a finite Galois intermediate extension $E/K$ for which $N=\operatorname{Gal}(K_S/E)\subseteq U$. Restriction $q:G\to\operatorname{Gal}(E/K)$ is surjective and $U=q^{-1}(qU)$. The fixed field of $N$ in $K_S$ is $E$. Indeed $K_S/E$ is Galois, so an element outside $E$ has another conjugate over $E$, and the embedding sending it to that conjugate extends to an automorphism of $K_S$. Consequently $K_S^U=E^{qU}$. Finite Galois correspondence gives

$$
 L=E^{qU},\qquad \operatorname{Gal}(E/L)=qU,\qquad
 [L:K]=[\operatorname{Gal}(E/K):qU]=[G:U].
$$

The upstairs fixing group of $L$ is $q^{-1}(\operatorname{Gal}(E/L))=U$. Any intermediate field with fixing group $U$ equals $K_S^U$ by the conjugate argument over that field. Order reversal follows from the pointwise definition. Normality of $U$ is equivalent to normality of $qU$, and finite Galois correspondence yields the group quotient in precisely that case.

The archive uses the closed-subgroup fixed-field equality, followed by the openness and finite-degree equivalence. It maps the resulting intermediate field of $K_S$ into $\Omega$, uses the tower formula for finiteness over $\mathbb Q$, and restricts scalars to represent the result as a `FiniteSubfield`. For a subset $T$ equal to the image of $L$ in $K_S$, fixing $T$ gives pointwise fixing on $L$ by membership of every image in $T$. The converse uses a preimage for every element of $T$. The image lemma identifies the resulting open subgroup with the original $U$. $\square$

**Lemma 18.3.23b (the same places and local maps) [Lean, read].** For $L=K_S^U$, $S_L$ is finite. Under the natural topological isomorphism $\theta:U\to G_{L,S_L}$ there is a bijection $e:(v,UxD_v)\mapsto w$ onto $S_L$. For the embeddings chosen from the same representatives there are continuous group isomorphisms $\psi_{v,x}:H_{v,x}\to G_{L_w}$ with

$$
 \theta\lambda_{v,x}=\lambda^L_w\psi_{v,x}.
$$

**Proof.** A prime of $K$ has finitely many primes above it in $L$. In the archive the height-one primes inject into the set of ideals, and the finite set of prime ideals over $\mathfrak p_v$ pulls back to the fibre over $v$. The finite union gives $S_L$. Lying over makes each fibre nonempty. A rational integer lying in $\mathfrak p_w$ lies in its contraction, which gives the coverage implication at $\ell$ used by the consumer.

The maximal extensions $L_{S_L}$ and $K_S$ have the same elements in $\Omega$. Outside $S$ the ramification index in a finite tower is the product of the two indices, and the residue extensions are separable. Since $L/K$ is unramified outside $S$, a finite extension of $L$ unramified outside $S_L$ is unramified over $K$ outside $S$. Conversely, for an element of $K_S$ take a finite subextension containing the element and $L$. Its extension over $L$ is unramified outside $S_L$ by the tower formula. Here finite unramified subextensions are closed under compositum, since their completions embed in the corresponding maximal unramified local extensions. The groups in $\theta$ act by the same automorphisms on the common field fixing $L$. Stabilizers of finite sets of elements give the same topology.

Use the finite Galois layer $E/K$ from Theorem 18.3.23a. For the prime $P$ of $E$ induced by $\iota_v$, the image $q(D_v)$ equals the decomposition group $D(P)$. This follows from surjective restriction of the local absolute Galois group to the finite Galois completion $E_P/K_v$. Surjectivity of $q$, with $\ker q\subseteq U$, identifies the two sets of double cosets.

$$
(U\setminus G)/D_v \ \simeq\ (q(U)\setminus \operatorname{Gal}(E/K))/D(P).
$$

Indeed equality of two double cosets downstairs lifts using one element each of $U$ and $D_v$, and the residual factor in $\ker q$ can be absorbed on the left. The primes of $E$ over $v$ form $\operatorname{Gal}(E/K)/D(P)$ via $g\mapsto gP$. Each fibre of contraction to $L$ is one orbit of $\operatorname{Gal}(E/L)=qU$ by Galois transitivity. So $UxD_v$ corresponds to $\left.q(x)P\right|_{L}$, and its stabilizer in $qU$ at $q(x)P$ is $qU\cap q(x)D(P)q(x)^{-1}$. By inversion one can instead use $D(P)$-orbits on the embeddings $\operatorname{Gal}(E/K)/qU$ of $L$.

To obtain the full local group, embed $L$ into $\overline{K_v}$ by $j_x=\iota_v\circ x^{-1}|_L$, using $\iota_v$ on $K_S$. The induced place is $w$. The relation $\iota_v\lambda_v(\sigma)=\sigma\iota_v$ on $K_S$ shows that fixing $j_x(L)$ pointwise is equivalent to $\sigma\in H_{v,x}$. By continuity it is equivalent to fixing its closure, the embedded completion $L_w$. Choose an algebraic closure identification $b_x:\overline{K_v}\simeq\overline{L_w}$ extending the completion identification. Conjugation by $b_x$ gives $\psi_{v,x}$ with continuous inverse, since it carries finite-set stabilizers to finite-set stabilizers. Lift $x$ to $\widetilde x\in\operatorname{Gal}(\Omega/K)$ and take $\iota^L_w=b_x\iota_v\widetilde x^{-1}$, an $L$-embedding. For $a\in K_S$ and $\sigma\in H_{v,x}$,

$$
\begin{aligned}
 \psi_{v,x}(\sigma)\iota^L_w(a)
 &= b_x\sigma\iota_v(x^{-1}a)\\
 &= b_x\iota_v\bigl(\lambda_v(\sigma)x^{-1}a\bigr)\\
 &= \iota^L_w\bigl(x\lambda_v(\sigma)x^{-1}a\bigr).
\end{aligned}
$$

This is the restriction equation. In `gsBaseChange_mackeyFamily_eq` the first equality is supplied by `gsBaseChange_mackeyFamily`. The proof then rewrites the upper local map using the value of `gsMackeyEmbeddings` at $e(v,UxD_v)$, which returns the embedding built from the same representative. The finite quotient above proves the indexing bijection in textbook terms. The archive constructs the bijection by comparing local embeddings inducing the same place. $\square$

**Changing choices.** If $x'=ux\lambda_v(h)$ with $u\in U$ and $h\in H_v$, then $j_{x'}=h^{-1}j_x$ on $L$. The induced place is unchanged. Conjugation by $h^{-1}$ identifies $H_{v,x}$ with $H_{v,x'}$, and

$$
 \lambda_{v,x'}(h^{-1}\sigma h)=u\lambda_{v,x}(\sigma)u^{-1}.
$$

A different lower embedding at $v$ differs on $K_S$ by precomposition with some $g\in G$. Then $\lambda_v'=g^{-1}\lambda_v g$, $D_v'=g^{-1}D_vg$, and the coordinate $UxD_v$ is represented by $UxgD_v'$. Two $L$-embeddings into the same upper local closure over $w$ likewise differ on $L_{S_L}$ by precomposition with some $g\in G_{L,S_L}$. This conjugates the map in the target. For $\lambda'=g^{-1}\lambda g$ the action of $g^{-1}$ identifies the $\lambda$-pullback of a module with its $\lambda'$-pullback. These isomorphisms identify the local restriction maps one coordinate at a time. To compare whole restriction diagrams when the conjugations vary with the place, keep the global map fixed and use the conjugation homotopy at each coordinate. In the bar construction, conjugate homomorphisms are functors between one-object group categories related by the natural transformation given by the conjugating element. Inserting that element at each possible position in a bar simplex with alternating signs gives the cochain homotopy, with the same coefficient action at the two ends. The product of these homotopies gives a map of restriction fibres. The two vertical cochain maps induce isomorphisms, so the long exact sequence of the fibre gives an isomorphism on compact cohomology. Changing the identification of completions also conjugates their local absolute groups. The archive's `exists_gsBaseChange_mackeyFamily_eq` gives the upper embedding comparison in this conjugate form. The literal equality in the lemma uses the matched embeddings.

**Theorem 18.3.23c (compact transport and the cup product) [Lean, read].** Let $A'$ be a discrete module for $G_{L,S_L}$ over a commutative ring, $A$ a discrete module for $U$, and $f:\theta^\ast A'\simeq A$ equivariant. In every degree $a$ there is an isomorphism $e_{A,a}:H_c^a(G_{L,S_L},S_L;A')\to H_c^a(U,\mathrm{Mackey};A)$. For discrete integral modules $D',D$ killed by the same positive $n$ and identified by such an $f$, use $R=\mathbb Z/n\mathbb Z$ with trivial action on both groups. Let $e_{R,q}:H^q(G_{L,S_L},R)\to H^q(U,R)$ denote ordinary pullback. With scalar pairing $d\otimes b\mapsto bd$, the compact-first cup satisfies

$$
 e_{D,p+q}(y\smile x)=e_{D,p}(y)\smile e_{R,q}(x),\qquad
 y\in H_c^p(G_{L,S_L},S_L;D'),\quad x\in H^q(G_{L,S_L},R).
$$

**Proof.** On global continuous cochains pull back arguments along $\theta$ and apply $f$ to values. On local cochains take the coordinate $w=e(v,UxD_v)$, pull back arguments along $\psi_{v,x}$, and apply $f$. The local restriction equation makes the two composites equal at every coordinate. The maps commute with the differentials by equivariance. The inverse group isomorphisms, inverse numbering, and $f^{-1}$ give inverse cochain maps. In the mapping fibre a degree-$a$ cochain is a global cochain of degree $a$ and a local family of degree $a-1$, with negative degrees zero. The two maps applied componentwise commute with the fibre differential $(a,b)\mapsto(da,\operatorname{res}(a)-db)$. In the archive the same restriction square gives a mapping-cocone map using the equality homotopy. The isomorphisms on global and local cohomology give the isomorphism on compact cohomology. The constructor `gsMackeyCompactIso` passes both families, $\theta$, $e$, the $\psi_{v,x}$, the commuting equation and $f$ to this construction.

The ordinary coefficient comparison is the identity on $R$. If $b\in R$ has integer representative $m$, then $f(bd)=f(md)=mf(d)=bf(d)$, independently of the representative since $n$ kills both modules. This checks the pairing on pure tensors. In the cochain cup, the first block of arguments supplies the first value and acts through its product on the value from the second block. Pullback respects that product, and equivariance and the pure-tensor identity respect the paired value. This applies to both global and local cochains. In the indicated fibre convention the compact-first product with an ordinary cochain $z$ is $(a\smile z,b\smile\operatorname{res}(z))$. If the fibre input has degree $p$, the Leibniz identity for this operation follows from the usual identity on global cochains and, on the local term, from $-\!(-1)^{p-1}=(-1)^p$. Thus the operation descends to cohomology, where it commutes with transport. The archive checks scalar multiplication in `zmodMulPairingRight_res`, then applies global and coordinatewise local cochain cup naturality in `compactTransport_compactCupRes`. The correction terms are zero for the equality homotopies. The specialization uses $f$ on the compact input and output and the identity scalar comparison on the ordinary input. $\square$

**Reading the square in both examples.** In the cubic example at $n=3$, the coefficient is the additive form of the actual $\mu_3$ in the common maximal extension. Complex conjugation belongs to $U$ and moves $\zeta_3$, so the coefficient action must be carried along $\theta$. For compact degree $2$ and ordinary degree $1$, the two local cups at $5$ use the degree-$1$ and degree-$2$ completions, and the single cup at $7$ uses the degree-$3$ completion. Each local part pairs degree-one cochains and lands in degree two. The same operation in the cyclic example at the split prime $7$ uses the two separate degree-$1$ completions and the same trivial scalar module. The target in both cases is transported by $e_{D,3}$. The cyclic example also satisfies the roots-fixing hypothesis for the upper-field arithmetic pairing.

For explicit local values, identify the additive roots with $\mathbb Z/3\mathbb Z$ by $\zeta_3\mapsto 1$. In Example 2 choose the compact boundary of the local degree-one class supported at the first place over $7$ and represented there by the scalar character just computed, with values now in the roots module. In the fibre convention its representative has zero global component and local cochain $b$ in degree $1$. At a Frobenius lift $\tau$ the value $b(\tau)$ is $2$ in the first coordinate and $0$ in the second. The output cochain at $(\tau,\tau)$ has first value $2\cdot 2=1\bmod 3$ and second value $0$. Reindexing gives the same values in the two corresponding Mackey coordinates.

In Example 1 choose the ordinary character obtained from $\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb Q(\zeta_9)/\mathbb Q)$ by quotienting by complex conjugation and sending the class of $4$ to $1$. It restricts to $G_{L,S_L}$ because the compositum is unramified outside $S_L$. At the degree-one place over $5$ its Frobenius value is $1$ since $5=-4\bmod 9$. A local roots cochain $b(\sigma)=\sigma(1)-1$ uses the additive generator $1$, with action by the cyclotomic character. At a Frobenius lift $\tau$ it has value $2-1=1\bmod 3$, since $5=2\bmod 3$. With the fibre input $(0,b)$ supported at this single coordinate the output at $(\tau,\tau)$ is $1\cdot 1=1\bmod 3$. The scalar action on the second factor is trivial even though the first coefficient has nontrivial action. These are cochain values, and this second $b$ is a coboundary. The calculation tracks the operation and the reindexing on representatives.

**The actual consumers.** `BaseMain` starts with every open $U\leq W_0$, where $W_0$ fixes the $n$th roots in $K_S$. It rewrites $U$ by Theorem 18.3.23a and retains the inclusion in $W_0$. The next wrapper builds the finite nonempty $S_L$, transfers coverage of primes over $\ell$, supplies the roots in $L$ and the odd-or-totally-complex guard, and invokes the inherited upper-field package with `gsMackeyEmbeddings`. It supplies the trace $T_L$ and its complementary-degree adjoints. The `BaseLevel` consumer calls the ordinary degree $r$ and compact degree $s$, so its cup uses $s+r=3$ from $r+s=3$. Its arrows are $e_X=e_{R,r}$, $e_Y=e_{D,s}$ and $e_W=e_{D,3}$, from the upper field to the subgroup model. The comparison $f$ is `gsRootsOfUnityIso.symm`, from the upper roots pulled back along $\theta$ to the lower roots restricted to $U$. On the subgroup-side compact degree three, the traces are $t_K\circ\operatorname{cores}_U$ and $T_L\circ e_W^{-1}$. The inherited trace argument relates them by a unit of $R$, enough to transfer bijectivity. Sections 18.3.21–22 give the surjectivity and four degree branches.

**Removing hypotheses.** With $K=\mathbb Q$ and $S=\{3\}$, put $M=\bigcup_{a\geq1}\mathbb Q(\zeta_{3^a})$. Every layer is unramified outside $3$ and has degree $2\cdot 3^{a-1}$. The closed normal subgroup $\operatorname{Gal}(K_S/M)$ fixes exactly $M$. Its index is at least $2\cdot3^{a-1}$ for every $a$, so it cannot be open. No finite intermediate field realizes this subgroup as a pointwise fixing group. Example 1 shows the distinct effect of dropping normality from the quotient assertion. The finite field still exists, but its three-element coset set has no quotient group structure induced from $G$.

**Sources and status.** Milne, *Fields and Galois Theory*, v5.10, Proposition 7.6 (p. 95), Proposition 7.12 (p. 97) and Theorem 7.13 (p. 98) give the Krull basis and infinite correspondence, including the openness and normality clauses. The finite reduction uses Theorem 3.17 (pp. 39–40). [Author PDF](https://www.jmilne.org/math/CourseNotes/FT.pdf)

Milne, *Algebraic Number Theory*, v3.08, Theorem 3.34 (p. 59) gives finite factorization and the degree sum, Proposition 8.2 (p. 136) gives the completion factors for the finite separable extension $L/K$ at the discrete nonarchimedean place $v$. Propositions 8.10 and 8.13(a) (pp. 139, 141) give the decomposition group in the finite Galois layer and its intersection with the fixing subgroup. Corollaries 7.51–7.52 (p. 128) give the local unramified extensions inside a given finite extension and inside the algebraic closure, respectively. Corollary 8.22 and Theorem 8.23 (p. 145) give the squarefree reduction and cycle comparison. Theorem 6.4 (pp. 99–100) gives cyclotomic degree and ramification. [Author PDF](https://www.jmilne.org/math/CourseNotes/ANT.pdf)

The named production bodies were read at local R3. The proofs and examples here are an author-level exposition. Kernel replay, exported `Main` reachability and external review remain [Open]. The arithmetic application retains the hypotheses of the fixed-prime-power consumer. The resulting scalar adjoints are the input to [§18.3.25](#18325-from-coinduced-coefficients-to-compact-duality-at-a-fixed-exponent) and then to [§18.3.24](#18324-combining-prime-power-compact-pairings-on-the-same-module). ### 18.3.24 Combining prime-power compact pairings on the same module

**Motivation.** At a prime power the open-subgroup argument starts from a scalar pairing over the subgroups below a roots-fixing open subgroup. [§18.3.21](#18321-from-the-coefficient-trace-to-surjective-compact-corestriction) gives the compact Mackey comparison and surjective degree-three corestriction. [§18.3.22](#18322-the-upper-field-root-of-unity-pairing) provides the upper-field trace and all four scalar adjoints at the same prime power. [§18.3.23](#18323-open-subgroups-larger-fixed-fields-and-the-local-family) realizes every such subgroup as a fixed-field group, identifies its local family and transports the compact-first cup. Together they furnish the scalar input to the fixed-exponent dévissage in [§18.3.25](#18325-from-coinduced-coefficients-to-compact-duality-at-a-fixed-exponent). Its output at that prime power can be applied to a primary summand of a larger coefficient module. To put the summands back together, each pairing has to use the invariant of the original finite-place family. The assembled adjoint supplies condition 2 of [§18.3.20](#18320-from-compact-support-to-the-nine-term-poitoutate-sequence), where the nine-term row and kernel pairing use it together with the other six conditions. For instance, the class of $1$ in $\mathbb Z/3\mathbb Z$ has invariant $1/3$ in $\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z$. As a value in $\mathbb Z/15\mathbb Z$, it must therefore be represented by $5$.

**Example 1 (exponent 15).** Take $K=\mathbb Q$, $S=\{(3),(5)\}$, the original completion embeddings, and the trivial $G_{K,S}$-module $M=\mathbb Z/15\mathbb Z$. Work in $(r,s)=(0,3)$. Evaluation at $1$ identifies the roots-valued dual of $M$ with $\mu_{15}$, with its cyclotomic action. Take $x=7$ in $H^0(G_{K,S},M)$. Choose a local roots class at $(3)$ of invariant $11/15$ and zero at $(5)$, and let $y$ be the corresponding compact boundary class under the identification by evaluation at $1$. These local lifts exist by Kummer and Hilbert 90: $H^2(K_v,\mu_m)\to \operatorname{Br}(K_v)$ identifies the source with the $m$-torsion, and the normalized local invariant identifies that torsion with $(1/m)\mathbb Z/\mathbb Z$. For an explicit Brauer representative, take the unramified extension of degree $m$, its arithmetic Frobenius, and the cyclic algebra with parameter $\pi^k$, where $\pi$ is a uniformizer. Its valuation cocycle is $k\lfloor(i+j)/m\rfloor$ for Frobenius exponents $0\le i,j<m$. This is the cocycle of the boundary of the character sending Frobenius to $k/m$, so its invariant is $k/m$. Here one can use $m=15$, $k=11$, $\pi=3$. The descended trace has $\lambda_{15}(y)=11$, so $y$ is nonzero. [Milne, *Arithmetic Duality Theorems*, I, Example 1.6(b) and §2](https://www.jmilne.org/math/Books/ADTnot.pdf)

The identity $2\cdot3-5=1$ gives the idempotents $e_3=-5\equiv10$ and $e_5=6$. They square to themselves, have product $60\equiv0$, and sum to $1$ modulo $15$. Thus $x_3=e_3x=10$, $x_5=e_5x=12$. In each summand use $e_q$ as a cyclic generator, so $x_3=1e_3$ and $x_5=2e_5$. The two dual coordinates restrict a functional on $M$ along the inclusions of those generators. Their compact invariant numerators at level $15$ are $11e_3=5$ and $11e_5=6$ modulo $15$. They have level-$3$ and level-$5$ numerators $1$ and $2$, since the value inclusions multiply by $15/3=5$ and $15/5=3$. The two characters on the small compact groups are multiplication on trace coordinates by $1$ modulo $3$ and $2$ modulo $5$. On the chosen coordinates they give $1$ and $4$, hence values $1/3$ and $4/5$ in $\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z$. The level-$15$ numerators are $5$ and $12$. The mixed products vanish by $e_3e_5=0$. Their sum is $17\equiv2$, agreeing with $7\cdot11=77\equiv2$. In the reverse direction the character $\chi(w)=7\lambda_{15}(w)$ restricts on the dual coordinates to the same small characters, and the recovered ordinary class is $1e_3+2e_5=22\equiv7$.

**Example 2 (exponent $8\cdot9$).** Take $K=\mathbb Q(i)$, the finite places over $2,3$ for $S$, and $M=\mathbb Z/72\mathbb Z$ with trivial action. This field is totally complex. Use $(r,s)=(0,3)$ again. Take $x=23$ and $y$ the compact boundary of the tuple of local roots classes with invariants $17/72$ at the place over $2$ and zero elsewhere. At $2$ the uniformizer is $1+i$, since $2=-i(1+i)^2$ in $\mathbb Z[i]$. The degree-$72$ unramified cyclic algebra with parameter $(1+i)^{17}$ gives the required local Brauer class by the same valuation calculation. Now $\lambda_{72}(y)=17$.

From $-8+9=1$ obtain $e_8=9$ and $e_9=-8\equiv64$. Their squares reduce to $9,64$, their product is zero modulo $72$, and their sum reduces to $1$. The ordinary coefficients are $x_8=7e_8=63$, $x_9=5e_9=32$. The compact dual coordinates have trace numerators $17e_8=9$ and $17e_9=8$ modulo $72$. They restrict back to $1$ and $1$ in the small trace coordinates, because the value inclusions multiply by $9$ and $8$. The small characters are multiplication by $7$ modulo $8$ and $5$ modulo $9$, giving paired values $7/8$ and $5/9$. Their level-$72$ numerators are $63$ and $40$. The mixed products vanish, and $63+40=103\equiv31\equiv23\cdot17$ modulo $72$. Restricting $\chi(w)=23\lambda_{72}(w)$ and using the two small adjoints in reverse recovers $7e_8+5e_9=95\equiv23$.

In both examples the actual roots lie in $K_S$ by cyclotomic ramification. The roots-fixing open subgroups can be taken to fix $\mathbb Q(\zeta_3)$ and $\mathbb Q(\zeta_5)$ in the first example, with indices $2,4$, and $\mathbb Q(\zeta_8)$ and $\mathbb Q(\zeta_9,i)$ in the second, with indices $2,6$ over $\mathbb Q(i)$. For the last index, $\mathbb Q(\zeta_9)$ is unramified at $2$ and therefore cannot contain $\mathbb Q(i)$. The place and roots conditions on every open subgroup below these follow from the fixed-field and Mackey comparison: the larger fixed field contains the same roots, the places above $S$ contain all places above $p$, and total complexity passes up the tower. The subgroup trace in the scalar adjoint is the original trace after compact corestriction. The base adjoints and the arithmetic vanishing are used here at their supplied interfaces. [Milne, *Algebraic Number Theory*, Theorem 6.4](https://www.jmilne.org/math/CourseNotes/ANT.pdf)

In these scalar examples the trace coordinate gives a character on the entire compact group. The theorem below also shows that the trace coordinate determines the compact class: the adjoint identifies its finite dual with $R_N$, and a finite group killed by $N$ has the same size as its $R_N$-valued dual. The trace is onto by the single-place lifts, so it is an isomorphism here.

**Definitions and hypotheses.** Fix a finite subfield $K$ of $\overline{\mathbb Q}$, a single finite set $S$ of finite places, and one $K$-embedding $\iota_v:\overline{\mathbb Q}\to\overline{K_v}$ for each $v\in S$. Let $G=G_{K,S}$ be the Galois group of the maximal extension $K_S$ unramified at finite places outside $S$, and let $\phi_v:G_{K_v}\to G$ be the maps induced by these embeddings. Write $H_c^s(A)$ for the compact cohomology of this family with coefficients $A$, computed by the mapping fibre of restriction. In degree $s$ its cochains are $(a,b)$ with $a$ global in degree $s$ and $b$ local in degree $s-1$, differential $(da,\mathrm{res}(a)-db)$, and negative-degree terms zero.

Fix $N>0$. Every finite place above every rational prime dividing $N$ lies in $S$, equivalently $N\in\mathfrak p_v$ implies $v\in S$. Assume $N$ is odd or $K$ is totally complex. For each prime $p\mid N$ put $q_p=p^{v_p(N)}$. These two arithmetic guards pass to every positive divisor $d$ of $N$: multiply $d$ by $N/d$ in each place ideal, and a divisor of an odd number is odd. The group, the embeddings, and the local family stay fixed. Set $R_d=\mathbb Z/d\mathbb Z$, $D_d=\mu_d(K_S)$ in additive notation, and $M^{\vee_d}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb Z}(M,D_d)$ with action $(gf)(m)=g f(g^{-1}m)$. The root counting supply identifies the underlying additive group of $D_d$ with $R_d$. For finite discrete $M$ this dual is finite and discrete: the actions on both finite sets have open kernels and the intersection fixes every function.

For each $d$, let $\lambda_d:H_c^3(D_d)\to R_d$ be the normalized compact trace called *gsCompactInv*, and let $\operatorname{inv}_{d,v}:H^2(K_v,D_d)\to\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z$ be *gsLocalInvRootsQZ*. Write $j_{q,N}:D_q\hookrightarrow D_N$ for the actual root inclusion *gsRootsIncl*. Write $\epsilon_d:R_d\hookrightarrow \mathbb Q/\mathbb Z$ for *zmodToQZ*, $[a]_d\mapsto a/d+\mathbb Z$. Its image is the $d$-torsion. Let $\partial_d$ be the local-to-compact boundary from the finite product of local degree-two groups. The supplied ordinary vanishing $H^3(G,D_d)=0$ and the fibre exact sequence make $\partial_d$ onto. The trace is the descent of the local sum, so
$$
\epsilon_d\lambda_d(\partial_d\xi)=\sum_{v\in S}\operatorname{inv}_{d,v}(\xi_v).
$$
These are the maps for the original coefficient modules and embeddings. [A2, RestrictedRamification/BaseDefs.lean:74–78,91–155,284–320; PoitouTate/ZmodToQZCompactCohomologyResMapOfLocalInv.lean:112–136,144–182.]

The exact prime-power input is the following. For each $p\mid N$ there exists an open $U_{0,p}\le G$ with *CompactDualityHypotheses* for $\phi,D_{q_p},\lambda_{q_p}$. The record supplies the additive identification of $D_{q_p}$ with $R_{q_p}$, vanishing of $H_c^4(A)$ for **every** finite discrete $G$-module $A$ killed by $q_p$, and, for **every** open $U\le U_{0,p}$ and **every** $r+s=3$, bijectivity of the scalar adjoint
$$
H^r(U,R_{q_p})\longrightarrow
\operatorname{Hom}\bigl(H_c^s(U,\mathrm{Mackey};D_{q_p}|_U),R_{q_p}\bigr)
$$
defined by the compact-first scalar cup and $\lambda_{q_p}$ after compact corestriction. The scalar coefficient on $U$ is trivial. The subgroup may depend on $p$. The fixed-prime-power dévissage proved in [§18.3.25](#18325-from-coinduced-coefficients-to-compact-duality-at-a-fixed-exponent) supplies from this record the roots-evaluation adjoint at level $q_p$ on **every** finite discrete $G$-module killed by $q_p$. We use that theorem, together with the fixed-field, Mackey and cup transport that supply the record, at their stated interfaces. [A2, PoincarePairs/IsDiscreteRepResHomCoindRes.lean:111–144; PoincarePairs.lean:222–232; PoincarePairs/Devissage.lean:430–446; RestrictedRamification/BaseMain.lean:23–64.]

**Lemma (comparison at a divisor).** For every positive $q$ dividing $N$, the normalized traces satisfy
$$
\epsilon_N\lambda_N((j_{q,N})_*z)=\epsilon_q\lambda_q(z)
\qquad(z\in H_c^3(D_q)).
$$

**Proof.** At a place $v$, the two coefficient maps to $\overline{K_v}^{\times}$ agree after first including $D_q$ into $D_N$. Both take a root to its image under the same $\iota_v$. Applying degree-two cohomology and the same Brauer invariant gives $\operatorname{inv}_{N,v}\circ (j_{q,N})_{v,*}=\operatorname{inv}_{q,v}$. This is the equality of coefficient maps in *gsLocalInvRootsQZ_comp_gsRootsIncl*. The degree-two cohomology map is functorial, including the scalar-universe comparison used in the definition of the local invariant. For any $z$ choose a tuple $\xi$ with $z=\partial_q\xi$. Applying a coefficient homomorphism to both components of the restriction fibre commutes with its differential and with local-to-compact. Therefore $(j_{q,N})_*z=\partial_N((j_{q,N})_{v,*}\xi_v)_v$. The displayed identities for the two traces, followed by the local comparison at each $v$, prove the lemma for all $z$. Equivalently $\lambda_N((j_{q,N})_*z)=(N/q)\lambda_q(z)$ with the indicated value map into $R_N$. [A2, PoitouTate/ZmodToQZCompactCohomologyResMapOfLocalInv.lean:41–71,127–136,144–182; PoitouTate/BrauerInvZOfIsSepClosureCohomologyULiftIntAddEqu.lean:189–230.]

**Lemma (a primary adjoint with the common target).** For a positive divisor $q$ of $N$ and a finite discrete $G$-module $X$ killed by $q$, put $J:X^{\vee_q}\to X^{\vee_N}$, $f\mapsto j_{q,N}\circ f$. Then $J$ is an equivariant isomorphism. For $r+s=3$ let $A_d^X$ be the additive adjoint
$$
A_d^X:H^r(G,X)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}\bigl(H_c^s(X^{\vee_d}),R_d\bigr),
\qquad A_d^X(x)(y)=\lambda_d(y\smile x).
$$
The cup has the compact factor first and uses $f\otimes m\mapsto f(m)$. If $A_q^X$ is bijective, $A_N^X$ is bijective.

**Proof.** The image of every $f:X\to D_N$ is killed by $q$, and $D_N[q]=j_{q,N}(D_q)$ because $D_N$ is cyclic of order $N$ and $D_q$ has order $q$. Thus each $f$ factors uniquely through $j_{q,N}$. The factorization is equivariant for the displayed dual actions. The archive obtains the same isomorphism by bounding the size of the larger dual by $|X|$, then comparing $|X|$ with the size of the small double dual and the size of the small dual. The induced compact map $J_*$ has inverse induced by $J^{-1}$.

On pure tensors, evaluating $(Jf,m)$ gives the inclusion of the evaluation of $(f,m)$. Cup naturality applies this square to the same global cochain and each restricted cochain. The compact fibre cup applies it to both components $(a\smile x,b\smile\operatorname{res}x)$, hence gives $(J_*y')\smile x=(j_{q,N})_*(y'\smile x)$. By the trace comparison,
$$
\epsilon_N A_N^X(x)(J_*y')=\epsilon_q A_q^X(x)(y').
$$
For injectivity, equality of two large adjoints gives equality after evaluation on every $J_*y'$ and after embedding the values in $\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z$. Injectivity of $\epsilon_q$ and of $A_q^X$ gives equality of the original ordinary classes. For surjectivity, take $\chi:H_c^s(X^{\vee_N})\to R_N$. The pullback $\epsilon_N\chi J_*$ takes values in $(\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z)[q]$. Indeed $q$ kills $X^{\vee_q}$ by $qf(m)=f(qm)$, and thus kills its compact cochains and compact classes. Pull the character back through $\epsilon_q$ to a unique $R_q$-valued character on $H_c^s(X^{\vee_q})$. This is *zmodOfQZ*. Let $x$ represent this character under $A_q^X$. The cup identity shows $\epsilon_N A_N^X(x)=\epsilon_N\chi$ on the image of $J_*$. Surjectivity of $J_*$ and injectivity of $\epsilon_N$ give $A_N^X(x)=\chi$. [A2, PoitouTate/Primary.lean:20–58,148–178; PoincareDuality/Torsion.lean:14–68.]

**Theorem 18.3.24 (assembly at the original exponent) [Lean, read].** Under the stated arithmetic and open-subgroup inputs, for every finite discrete $G$-module $M$ killed by $N$ and each $r,s\ge0$ with $r+s=3$, $A_N^M$ is bijective. Its invariant and its local family are the ones just defined.

**Proof.** First record the primary projections. For $p\mid N$, let $c_p=N/q_p$ and choose $u_p$ inverse to $c_p$ modulo $q_p$. Then $e_p=c_pu_p$ in $R_N$ has residue $1$ modulo $q_p$ and $0$ at every other prime power. Divisibility by their product proves $e_p^2=e_p$, $e_pe_\ell=0$ for $p\ne\ell$, and $\sum_pe_p=1$ when $N>1$. Consequently $M=\bigoplus_p M_p$ for $M_p=e_pM=M[q_p]$. All scalar maps commute with $G$. If $i_p:M_p\to M$ is inclusion and $\pi_p:M\to M_p$ is multiplication by $e_p$ into its image, the dual projection is $f\mapsto f i_p$ and the dual inclusion is $f_p\mapsto f_p\pi_p$. Their composites give the dual projections $f\mapsto f(e_p\,\cdot)=e_p f$. Each primary dual has image in $D_N[q_p]$, identified with $D_{q_p}$ through $j_{q_p,N}$. The ordinary cochains and both parts of the compact fibre split by the same equivariant maps. Thus their cohomology splits with the indicated directions. Off-diagonal cups vanish because $f_p\pi_p i_\ell=0$ if $p\ne\ell$. This is the finite Chinese remainder decomposition on the original module and its dual.

The production proof applies the decomposition two factors at a time, keeping the target $D_N,R_N,\lambda_N$ throughout induction on an annihilator $d\mid N$. Fix $r+s=3$ during this induction and allow **every** finite discrete coefficient killed by $d$. The case $d=0$ is excluded by $N>0$. For $d=1$ the coefficient is zero. If $N=1$ the source and character group are zero, and the adjoint is bijective. If $N>1$ the zero coefficient is also covered by the comparison for any prime dividing $N$. If $d=p^k$ with $k>0$, then $p\mid N$ and $k\le v_p(N)$, so $q_p$ kills the coefficient. Apply the supplied prime-power adjoint and the previous lemma.

For $d=ab$, where $a,b>1$ are coprime, write $\alpha a+\beta b=1$. Take $X_a=\beta bM$ and $X_b=\alpha aM$, killed by $a,b$ respectively because $abM=0$. These are finite discrete subrepresentations. Use inclusions $i_j$ and projections $p_j$ into the ranges, $j\in\{a,b\}$. The sum $\sum_j i_jp_j$ is the identity. Write $I_j,P_j$ for the induced maps on ordinary cohomology and $I_j^\vee,P_j^\vee$ for the maps on compact cohomology induced by precomposition with $i_j,p_j$. The latter go in the opposite directions. Evaluating at a vector checks the identity between the two evaluation maps, so cup naturality gives
$$
A_N^M(I_j u)(y)=A_N^{X_j}(u)(I_j^\vee y),\qquad
A_N^{X_j}(P_j x)(v)=A_N^M(x)(P_j^\vee v).
$$
Additivity of cohomology and of the contravariant dual implies
$$
x=\sum_j I_jP_j x,\qquad y=\sum_j P_j^\vee I_j^\vee y.
$$
If $A_N^M(x)=0$, the second pairing identity gives $A_N^{X_j}(P_j x)=0$ for each $j$. Inductive injectivity kills both $P_j x$, so the first decomposition kills $x$. For a character $\chi$ on the large compact group, choose $u_j$ giving $\chi P_j^\vee$ by inductive surjectivity. Then for $x=\sum_j I_j u_j$,
$$
A_N^M(x)(y)=\sum_j A_N^{X_j}(u_j)(I_j^\vee y)
=\sum_j\chi(P_j^\vee I_j^\vee y)=\chi(y).
$$
This proves surjectivity with a preimage on the original ordinary cohomology group. The divisor recursion applies because any composite with distinct prime divisors splits into smaller coprime factors. In Lean the *true* branch is the range of $\beta b$ and the *false* branch is the range of $\alpha a$. Evaluate at $d=N$ to finish. [A2, PoitouTate/Primary.lean:62–133,200–256; PoincarePairs/Natural.lean:239–300. Pinned Mathlib, Mathlib/Data/Nat/Factorization/Induction.lean:26–72 and Mathlib/RingTheory/Coprime/Lemmas.lean:38–54.]

**Failure when the normalizations are independent.** Keep the actual groups, modules, roots inclusions, and prime-power traces of Example 1, and replace the common trace by $\widetilde{\lambda}_{15}=3\lambda_{15}$. Every cup with the trivial coefficient $X=\mathbb Z/3\mathbb Z$ evaluates through $D_{15}[3]$. Its compact trace is killed by $3$, so the changed adjoint vanishes on all of $H^0(G,X)=\mathbb Z/3\mathbb Z$. The original small adjoint stays bijective. In particular if $\lambda_3(z)=1$, the normalized comparison would give $1/3$ at the two levels, whereas the new large trace gives zero. Such $z$ is a boundary of a local class with invariant $1/3$ at $(3)$. This disproves the inference using only the small adjoints and an arbitrary additive large trace. The change also violates the large trace's specified local-sum formula. Rescaling instead by a unit, for example $2$ modulo $15$, preserves large bijectivity but sends this same value to $2/3$ and breaks the normalized comparison. The normalization governs equality of these actual pairings.

**Failure at 2 over a real field.** Keep $N=72$ but take $K=\mathbb Q$ and $S=\{(2),(3)\}$, with the trivial module $M=\mathbb Z/72\mathbb Z$. The quotient of $G_{\mathbb Q,S}$ given by $\mathbb Q(i)$ is $C_2$ and complex conjugation gives it a section: its restriction to $\mathbb Q(i)$ is the nonidentity involution. Hence inflation from $C_2$ to $G_{\mathbb Q,S}$ in degree three is split injective. In the cyclic resolution with alternating $g-1$ and $1+g$, the trivial action gives $H^3(C_2,M)=M[2]=\{0,36\}$. So $H^3(G_{\mathbb Q,S},M)$ is nonzero. But for any coefficients $B$, restriction in degree zero from global invariants to the nonempty finite family of local invariants is injective, and consequently $H_c^0(B)=0$. The degree $(3,0)$ adjoint to this finite-place compact group has zero target and cannot be injective, for any additive choice of trace. This shows exactly where dropping the odd-or-totally-complex guard fails. It makes no assertion about retaining all the supplied compact-duality records in that case, and the real-place-completed theory uses a different compact complex. [Milne, *Arithmetic Duality Theorems*, I.4.10(c) and II §2](https://www.jmilne.org/math/Books/ADTnot.pdf)

**Remark (what the production record adds).** In *gsPoitouTateDualityHypotheses* the finite discrete module is chosen after the package is produced. Its compact-adjoint field uses *bijective_compactDualityMap_of_primePow* and calls the selected open-subgroup record at each exact $q_p$. Its remaining fields are the additive roots identification, finiteness of ordinary and compact cohomology in every natural degree, ordinary degree-three vanishing, the normalized local sum, and surjectivity of the local $(0,2)$ pairing. These use the archive's indicated arithmetic finiteness, dimension and local duality supplies. The local sum comparison also uses the arithmetic vanishing for the roots in both levels. *Deduction* passes the package to the compact adjoint, the Sha pairing and the nine-term sequence. The corresponding public wrappers supply the prime-power records via *gsBaseCase* on the same group and family. The other direct consumer is the degree-two cardinality identity in *ShaCount*. [A2, PoitouTate/ZmodToQZCompactCohomologyResMapOfLocalInv.lean:188–232; PoitouTate/General.lean:22–44; PoitouTate/Deduction.lean:34–67; PoitouTate/PoitouTateNineTermGs.lean:23–69; GlobalEulerCharacteristic/ShaCount.lean:101–107.]

**Attribution and reading scope.** Tate's ICM Theorem 3.1 gives the classical finite-kernel pairing and localization sequence and acknowledges Poitou's independent work. Milne I.4.10 uses finite modules whose order is invertible in the ring of $S$-integers with the archimedean terms included. Milne II.3.1(b) and Corollary 3.3 treat compact-support duality for constructible sheaves. For the corresponding pairing, use the locally constant sheaf for $M^{\vee_N}$ on $\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal O_{K,S}$ and its double dual $M$. The support condition makes $N$ invertible there. The modified archimedean terms vanish for odd torsion or a totally complex field. In the finite-place trace, each local term uses the same Brauer invariant under every roots inclusion. The duality is classical. The divisor induction, the choice of common target and the character lift above describe the specific implementation. The two examples and the separate proofs for the actual trace change and the actual reconstruction explain the pinned Alpöge–Claude implementation of this classical duality. The source reading is local and author-level. The deeper arithmetic supplies, declaration-level export reachability, local kernel replay and peer review have their own evidence requirements. ### 18.3.25 From coinduced coefficients to compact duality at a fixed exponent

**Motivation.** The prime-power assembly in §18.3.24 uses a result about every finite coefficient module at each fixed exponent. Its input from the upper fields is a family of scalar pairings on open subgroups. After Shapiro and the compact Mackey comparison, these give the pairings for coinduced coefficients. An arbitrary module embeds into a finite sum of these coinduced modules, but its quotient can have new cohomology. We will follow that quotient through the two connecting maps. The ordinary degree goes up by one as the complementary compact degree goes down by one. The trace and the restriction family are fixed throughout. [A2, PoincarePairs.lean:195–232 and PoincarePairs/Devissage.lean:137–233,253–446.]

**Two examples [Expository].** Use $R=\mathbb F_3$, $G=\langle g\mid g^3=1\rangle$, $D=R$ with trivial action, and two local maps, both $\mathrm{id}_G$. These are actual finite-group cochains for a restriction interface. The restriction is the nonzero diagonal. Write the compact coordinates as $(a;b_1,b_2)$. The map to the shifted local complex is $b_2-b_1$. Its chain section sends $b$ to $(0;0,b)$, and the differential on this summand is $-d$. Its complement consists of triples $(a;b,b)$ and is contractible by $(a;b,b)\mapsto(b;0,0)$, with this homotopy zero where the target degree is negative. Thus $H_c^j(X)=H^{j-1}(G,X)$ for $j\ge1$ under this difference, and $H_c^0(X)=0$. The compact-first cup with a global cochain acts on the difference by the ordinary local cup.

Use normalized inhomogeneous cochains (a positive-degree cochain vanishes when any argument is $1$). The same trace in both examples is
$$
t([(a;b_1,b_2)])=\sum_{i=0}^2(b_2-b_1)(g^i,g)
\qquad\text{in degree }3.
$$
Indeed, if $b_2-b_1=dh$ in degree two, then $(dh)(g^i,g)=h(g)-h(g^{i+1})+h(g^i)$ and the sum is zero in $R$. The carry $c(g^i,g^j)=\lfloor(i+j)/3\rfloor$ for $0\le i,j<3$ is a normalized two-cocycle: the two ways to sum three residues have the same total carry. Its trace on $(0;0,c)$ is $1$.

Let $I=R[G]=R[u]/(u^3)$ with $u=g-1$, and put $e=1$ and $v=u^2$. For a linear functional $f$ on $I$, choose $f(e)=f(u)=0$ and $f(v)=1$. The contragredient action is $(g f)(x)=f(g^{-1}x)$. We use $1+g+g^2=u^2$, $g^\ell e=e+\ell u+\binom{\ell}{2}v$, $g^\ell u=u+\ell v$, and $g^\ell v=v$. The cup of a dual-valued cochain $b$ of ordinary degree $k$ with $z$ evaluates $b(g_1,\ldots,g_k)$ on $(g_1\cdots g_k)z(g_{k+1},\ldots)$. The compact product uses this formula on each local coordinate.

1. **Even compact degree $s=2$.** Set $M=uI$, $Q=I/M=R$, with inclusion and quotient. The ordinary class $q=1$ in $H^0(G,Q)$ lifts to $e$. Its connecting cocycle is $a(g^j)=g^je-e=j u+\binom j2v$. For the compact class $y$ use $(0;0,\beta)$ with
   $$
   \beta(g^i)=\left.\sum_{k=0}^{i-1}g^k f\right|_M.
   $$
   If $B(g^i)=\sum_{k<i}g^k f$, the inhomogeneous differential is $g^i B(g^j)-B(g^{i+j})+B(g^i)=c(g^i,g^j)(1+g+g^2)f$. The norm of $f$ is $p^\vee(1)$, since it evaluates on $x$ as $f(u^2x)$, the constant coefficient of $x$. Hence $\beta$ is closed. Lifting $y$ using $B$ in its second local coordinate gives $\delta_c y=[(0;0,-c)]$, the minus coming from the shifted differential. Its cup with $q=1$ has trace $-1$. In the other product the second local coordinate at $(g^i,g^j)$ is
   $$
   \beta(g^i)(g^i a(g^j))=i\binom j2+j\,i(i+1)/2.
   $$
   Here division by $2$ is valid in $R$. At $j=1$ the three values for $i=0,1,2$ are $0,1,0$. The trace of $y\smile\delta q$ is $1$. These values check the sign $-(-1)^2=-1$ with the original $t$.

2. **Odd compact degree $s=1$.** Use the other coefficient sequence $M=Rv\hookrightarrow I\twoheadrightarrow Q=I/Rv$. The norm of $\bar e$ is zero in $Q$. For $q$ use the one-cocycle represented by $\sum_{k<i}g^k\bar e$ at $g^i$, lifted to $A(g^i)=\sum_{k<i}g^ke$. The same carry computation gives $dA=c\,v$, so $\delta q$ is represented by $c\,v$. Take $y=[(0;0,\eta)]$ where $\eta(v)=1$, a local invariant. Lifting $\eta$ by $f$ gives the dual compact connection represented by $(0;0,\kappa)$, where $\kappa(g^i)=f-g^if$ descends to $Q$. The local coordinates of $y\smile\delta q$ are $(0,c)$ and the trace is $1$. In $(\delta_c y)\smile q$ the second local coordinate evaluated at $(g^i,g^j)$ is
   $$
   f(g^i A(g^j))-f(A(g^j))=
   \sum_{k<j}\left(\binom{i+k}{2}-\binom{k}{2}\right).
   $$
   At $j=1$ the values are $0,0,1$, again giving trace $1$. The equality now uses $-(-1)^1=+1$. Both calculations use nonzero compact products, so the opposite sign fails in each case.

**Definitions and hypotheses.** Let $G$ be a compact topological group and $\phi=(\phi_v:H_v\to G)_v$ a fixed family of continuous homomorphisms of topological groups. Write $C^j(G,X)$ for continuous inhomogeneous cochains with values in the discrete $G$-module $X$ and $L^j(X)=\prod_v C^j(H_v,X|_{\phi_v})$. Put $H^j(X)=H^j(G,X)$. The notation $H_c^j(X)$ always refers to the same family $\phi$ and to the complex
$$
F^j(X)=C^j(G,X)\oplus L^{j-1}(X),\qquad
d_F(a,b)=(da,\operatorname{res}(a)-db),
$$
where $L^{-1}=0$. For $n>0$ put $R=\mathbb Z/n\mathbb Z$ and fix a discrete $G$-module $D$, an additive trace $t:H_c^3(D)\to R$, and one open subgroup $U_0\le G$. Write $X^\vee=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb Z}(X,D)$ with $(gf)(x)=g f(g^{-1}x)$. For $f:X\to Y$, the map $f^\vee:Y^\vee\to X^\vee$ is precomposition. For a compact group the coinduced module $C_U=\operatorname{Coind}_U^G R$ on a trivial $U$-module is finite and discrete for open $U$, with pointwise coefficients killed by $n$. Write the compact-first adjoint as
$$
A_X^{r,s}:H^r(X)\longrightarrow \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb Z}(H_c^s(X^\vee),R),
\qquad
A_X^{r,s}(x)(y)=t(y\smile x).
$$
The evaluation in this cup is $(f,x)\mapsto f(x)$. The hypotheses of *CompactGroupDualityHypotheses* are the following.

- The underlying additive group of $D$ is isomorphic to $R$.
- For **every** finite discrete $G$-module $X$ killed by $n$, $H_c^4(X)=0$.
- For **every** open $U\le U_0$ and **every** pair of natural degrees $r+s=3$, $A_{C_U}^{r,s}$ is bijective with these same $\phi,D,t$.

The additive identification implies that $D$ is finite and killed by $n$. Each dual of a finite discrete module is finite and discrete: intersect the stabilizers of each argument and of its value under the given functional. The duals and their compact cochains and cohomology are killed by $n$. This last fact is pointwise, including the full product of local cochains. [A2, PoincarePairs/Devissage.lean:169–199; PoincarePairs/IsDiscreteRepResHomCoindRes.lean:48–70; PoincareDuality/NsmulEqZeroOfNonemptyAddEquiv.lean:21–49,94–107; PoincarePairs/Natural.lean:137–147.]

**Lemma (finite coinduced enlargement and the reverse exact sequence) [Lean, read].** For every finite discrete $M$ killed by $n$ there is a short exact sequence $0\to M\xrightarrow{i}I\xrightarrow{p}Q\to0$ of finite discrete modules killed by $n$, with $A_I^{r,s}$ bijective in all four complementary degrees. Its dual sequence is $0\to Q^\vee\xrightarrow{p^\vee}I^\vee\xrightarrow{i^\vee}M^\vee\to0$.

**Proof.** Use the character separation and extension already supplied by *zmod_exists_apply_ne_zero* and *zmod_exists_comp_eq_of_ker_le*. In the latter, for $h:B\to C$ and $\chi:B\to R$ with $\ker h\subseteq\ker\chi$, the induced character on $\operatorname{im}h$ extends to $C$ provided $nC=0$. This follows by embedding $R$ as $(\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z)[n]$, extending the induced character using injectivity of the divisible group $\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z$, and using $nC=0$ to bring the extension back to $R$. A nonzero $a$ of order $d\mid n$ is separated by extending the map $a\mapsto 1/d$ on its cyclic subgroup and applying the same value restriction. The target group $C$ for an extension can be infinite.

Take $W=U_0\cap\bigcap_{m\in M}\operatorname{Stab}_G(m)$, which is open and contained in $U_0$. The finite set $J=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb Z}(M,R)$ indexes maps $M\to C_W$. The character $\chi$ is $W$-equivariant because both restricted actions are trivial on the relevant modules. The adjunction gives $m\mapsto(g\mapsto\chi(gm))$, whose value at $1$ is $\chi(m)$. Equality of two images in the biproduct $I=\bigoplus_{\chi\in J} C_W$ gives equality at $1$ in every component, so character separation makes $i$ injective. Compactness gives finite index for $W$, and the action on its finite coset set has open kernel. Thus $I$ is finite discrete and killed by $n$. The actual cokernel $Q=I/i(M)$ has the same three properties by lifting through the quotient: the stabilizer of a lift fixes its image, the image is finite, and $n$ kills every lift.

Additivity and naturality split $A_I^{r,s}$ into the component adjoints. Explicitly, evaluation on $\bigoplus C_W$ is the sum of component evaluations and the component projections for the coefficient become inclusions on the dual. The cochain maps for the finite sum give mutually inverse decomposition maps on both cohomologies. A zero adjoint has zero component adjoints by testing each compact component. A given character on the sum is the sum of its component restrictions, which are reached by the corresponding base adjoints. The same finite $I$ works for all four pairs.

In the reverse sequence $p^\vee$ is injective because $p$ is onto. A homomorphism on $I$ vanishing on $i(M)$ factors through $Q$, proving exactness at $I^\vee$. To lift a homomorphism $M\to D$ along $i^\vee$, identify the target additively with $R$ and extend along $i$ using $nI=0$. The extension is an element of the internal dual, whose elements are additive homomorphisms. The dual maps themselves are $G$-equivariant. This proves short exactness with the reverse arrow order. [A2, PoincarePairs/Devissage.lean:137–165,203–233; PoincarePairs/Natural.lean:239–307; PoincareDuality/Torsion.lean:72–96; PoincareDuality/TensorHomZmodDualIsoZmodMulPairing.lean:109–176.]

**Lemma (coefficient connections with the compact factor first) [Lean, read].** For this short exact sequence write $i_*,p_*$ for the maps on ordinary cohomology, and $i_c:H_c^j(I^\vee)\to H_c^j(M^\vee)$ and $p_c:H_c^j(Q^\vee)\to H_c^j(I^\vee)$ for the reversed maps. Let $\delta:H^r(Q)\to H^{r+1}(M)$ and $\delta_c:H_c^s(M^\vee)\to H_c^{s+1}(Q^\vee)$ be the coefficient connections. If $r+s=2$, then for all $q,y$ in their respective domains,
$$
A_M^{r+1,s}(\delta q)(y)=\epsilon_s A_Q^{r,s+1}(q)(\delta_c y),\qquad
\epsilon_s=-(-1)^s.
$$

**Proof.** We use the degreewise short exact cochain sequences and their exact positions inherited from the compact corestriction argument. On each restricted coefficient sequence the maps are the original coefficient maps. In degree $j$, a set section of a coefficient surjection lifts locally constant cochains by composition. Thus the fibre is degreewise exact too. Its coefficient boundary is represented by the differential of a lift, pulled back along the coefficient inclusion. This is the convention of *compactδRes*, distinct from the local-to-compact boundary $[b]\mapsto[(0,b)]$ associated to the restriction map itself.

On cochains the compact-first cup of $(a,b)$ with $z$ is $(a\smile z,b\smile\operatorname{res}z)$. The global differentials obey the cup Leibniz rule and restriction commutes with cup. In the local coordinate of $d_F((a,b)\smile z)$, with $(a,b)$ of degree $s$, the result is
$$
(\operatorname{res}a-db)\smile\operatorname{res}z
  +(-1)^s b\smile\operatorname{res}(dz).
$$
The sign follows from the minus on the differential of $b$, whose unshifted degree is $s-1$. At $s=0$ the local input $b$ is zero and the formula still holds.

Choose a compact cochain lift $y_2$ through $i^\vee$ and an ordinary cochain lift $q_2$ through $p$, both lifting closed representatives. Their differentials represent $p^\vee\delta_c y$ and $i\,\delta q$ with the corresponding closed cochain representatives understood. In the differential of $y_2\smile q_2$, both evaluation squares hold before and after restriction, since evaluation of a precomposition is evaluation on the image of the argument. The Leibniz identity therefore makes $(\delta_c y)\smile q+(-1)^s y\smile\delta q$ a coboundary. Applying the very same $t$ gives the claimed identity. Multiplication by $\epsilon_s$ is its own inverse. At any complementary pair the same evaluation square with a coefficient arrow $f:X\to Y$ also gives
$$
A_Y^{r,s}(f_*x)(y)=A_X^{r,s}(x)\bigl((f^\vee)_{c,*}y\bigr).
$$
[A2, PoincarePairs/Devissage.lean:24–70,81–129; CompactSupportCohomology/ConePairing.lean:29–93,100–116; CompactSupportCohomology/Pairing.lean:141–206; CompactSupportCohomology/CompactCupEqCompactCupRight.lean:166–231; PoincarePairs/Natural.lean:194–226,239–254.]

**Theorem 18.3.25 (compact duality at a fixed exponent) [Lean, read].** Under the three stated hypotheses, $A_M^{r,s}$ is bijective for every finite discrete $G$-module $M$ killed by $n$ and every pair $(r,s)$ of natural degrees with $r+s=3$.

**Proof.** In what follows, a subscript or superscript for degree is omitted only when the class being mapped specifies it. Use the enlargement sequence for $M$. Exactness gives $\ker p_*=\operatorname{im}i_*$ in each ordinary degree, $\ker i_*=\operatorname{im}\delta$ one degree higher, and $\delta p_*=0$. The compact sequence gives $\ker\delta_c=\operatorname{im}i_c$ and $\ker p_c$ in the next degree equal to $\operatorname{im}\delta_c$. In every degree $i_c p_c=0$, since $(p i)^\vee=i^\vee p^\vee=0$. We use these positions directly.

*Degree zero, injectivity for all coefficients.* If $A_M^{0,3}(x)=0$, naturality gives $A_I^{0,3}(i_*x)(z)=A_M^{0,3}(x)(i_cz)=0$ for every $z$. The enlargement pairing is injective, so $i_*x=0$. Since $H^0$ is the invariant subgroup, the underlying injection $i$ makes its restriction on invariants injective. Therefore $x=0$.

*Degree zero, surjectivity for all coefficients.* In the compact sequence in degree three the arrow following $i_c$ is $\delta_c:H_c^3(M^\vee)\to H_c^4(Q^\vee)$. The all-coefficient vanishing applies to $Q^\vee$, which is finite discrete and killed by $n$. Thus $i_c$ is onto. Given $\lambda:H_c^3(M^\vee)\to R$, take $v\in H^0(I)$ with $A_I^{0,3}(v)=\lambda i_c$. For every $z\in H_c^3(Q^\vee)$,
$$
A_Q^{0,3}(p_*v)(z)=A_I^{0,3}(v)(p_c z)=\lambda(i_c p_cz)=0.
$$
Degree-zero injectivity, already established for every coefficient including $Q$, makes $p_*v=0$. Exactness produces $x\in H^0(M)$ with $i_*x=v$. For any $y=i_cz$ naturality yields $A_M^{0,3}(x)(y)=A_I^{0,3}(v)(z)=\lambda(y)$, and all $y$ admit such a lift.

*Successor, injectivity for all coefficients in degree $r+1$.* Fix $r+s=2$ and assume bijectivity at $(r,s+1)$ for **every** finite discrete coefficient killed by $n$. If $A_M^{r+1,s}(x)=0$, its image in $I$ has zero adjoint by naturality and hence $i_*x=0$. Write $x=\delta q$ by ordinary exactness. For any $z\in\ker(p_c:H_c^{s+1}(Q^\vee)\to H_c^{s+1}(I^\vee))$, compact exactness gives $z=\delta_c y$. The connection identity and invertibility of $\epsilon_s$ give $A_Q^{r,s+1}(q)(z)=0$. Apply character extension along this $p_c$ to obtain $\psi:H_c^{s+1}(I^\vee)\to R$ with $\psi p_c=A_Q^{r,s+1}(q)$. Its target of extension $H_c^{s+1}(I^\vee)$ is killed by $n$. By surjectivity at $(r,s+1)$ on $I$, choose $v$ with $A_I^{r,s+1}(v)=\psi$. Naturality gives $A_Q^{r,s+1}(p_*v)=\psi p_c=A_Q^{r,s+1}(q)$. Injectivity at $(r,s+1)$ on $Q$ now gives $q=p_*v$. It follows that $x=\delta p_*v=0$.

*Successor, surjectivity for all coefficients in degree $r+1$.* Given $\lambda:H_c^s(M^\vee)\to R$, choose $v\in H^{r+1}(I)$ with $A_I^{r+1,s}(v)=\lambda i_c$. For each $z\in H_c^s(Q^\vee)$, naturality gives $A_Q^{r+1,s}(p_*v)(z)=\lambda(i_cp_cz)=0$. The successor injectivity just proved for every coefficient applies to $Q$ at **this** degree. Thus $p_*v=0$, so choose $x'\in H^{r+1}(M)$ with $i_*x'=v$. The difference $\nu=\lambda-A_M^{r+1,s}(x')$ vanishes on $\operatorname{im}i_c$ by naturality and the choice of $v$. This image is $\ker\delta_c$. Extend along $\delta_c$ to a character $\psi$ of $H_c^{s+1}(Q^\vee)$ satisfying $\psi\delta_c=\nu$. This extension uses $nH_c^{s+1}(Q^\vee)=0$. By the lower-degree surjectivity on $Q$, choose $q$ with $A_Q^{r,s+1}(q)=\psi$. Set $x=x'+\epsilon_s\delta q$. For every $y\in H_c^s(M^\vee)$,
$$
A_M^{r+1,s}(x)(y)=A_M^{r+1,s}(x')(y)
    +\epsilon_s^2\,\psi(\delta_c y)=\lambda(y).
$$

The sole induction variable is the ordinary degree, with the statement at each degree universally quantified over the coefficients before proceeding to the next; one cannot induct on $|M|$ or on the exponent because the cokernel $Q=I/i(M)$ is typically larger than $M$ while sharing the bound $nQ=0$. The construction first proves $(0,3)$ and then uses $s=2,1,0$ to prove $(1,2),(2,1),(3,0)$ respectively, with alternating correction signs $-1,+1,-1$. The last step uses $H_c^0$ and $\delta_c:H_c^0(M^\vee)\to H_c^1(Q^\vee)$, and the initial step uses $H_c^4(Q^\vee)=0$. Unlike ordinary degree-two local dévissage (*PoincareDuality/Devissage.lean*), which places the ordinary factor first, uses a single constant minus sign at $(0,2)\to(1,1)$, imposes no subgroup ceiling $U_0$, and closes top degree by biduality and finite-$H^2$ counting, the compact proof keeps $U\le U_0$, places the compact factor first, drives every positive ordinary degree through the reversed compact sequence, and needs no finiteness hypothesis on cohomology groups. These four pairs exhaust the nonnegative solutions of $r+s=3$. When $n=1$ the coefficient class consists of zero modules and the conclusion is immediate. [A2, PoincarePairs/Devissage.lean:253–310,316–410,430–446.]

**What the hypotheses control.** The four coinduced base pairings cannot be discarded as a family. Take $G=1$, the two identity local maps, $n=3$, $D=R$ and the unique trace to $R$ from $H_c^3(R)=0$. The fibre calculation above gives $H_c^4(X)=0$ for every finite module and the single open subgroup can be used for $U_0$. For $M=R$ the $(0,3)$ adjoint is $R\to0$. This keeps the other record fields and drops the coinduced bijectivity field.

There is also a specific obstruction to omitting the vanishing in the proof. In the first finite-group example, the coinduced $I^\vee$ has zero positive cohomology by ordinary Shapiro from $1$. Thus $H_c^3(I^\vee)=0$. For an explicit degree-three compact class, let $f_u$ be the functional on $I$ extracting the coefficient of $u$. Its restriction to $M=uI$ is invariant, since $g^\ell u=u+\ell v$. Thus the second local coordinate $c\,f_u|_M$ defines a compact cocycle. Its lift has second local coordinate $c\,f_u$. Since $f_u-g^if_u=i\,p^\vee(1)$ and $dc=0$, the second local coordinate of its compact boundary in $Q^\vee$ is the three-cocycle $(g^i,g^j,g^k)\mapsto i\,c(g^j,g^k)$. Its sum on $(g,g^j,g)$ over $j=0,1,2$ is $1$. For any scalar two-cochain $h$, the sum of $(dh)(g,g^j,g)=h(g^j,g)-h(g^{j+1},g)+h(g,g^{j+1})-h(g,g^j)$ is zero. The boundary is therefore nonzero and $i_c$ is not onto. This is a counterexample to the endpoint surjectivity without vanishing. The model also lacks the full coinduced base field of the record. Finally the exponent condition of the character extension has its own strict failure: on $3(\mathbb Z/9\mathbb Z)$ send $3$ to $1$ in $\mathbb Z/3\mathbb Z$. No character on $\mathbb Z/9\mathbb Z$ extends it, since every such character sends $3$ to zero.

**Arithmetic use and attribution.** *CompactDualityHypotheses.bijective_compactDualityMap* in *PoincarePairs.lean* consumes this theorem. Its preceding bridge uses ordinary Shapiro and the compact Mackey comparison to identify the coinduced adjoints with scalar adjoints on the subgroup, using $t$ after compact corestriction. The same $\phi,D,t,U_0$ pass to the present theorem. On the arithmetic side, *exists_compactDualityHypotheses_gs* [Lean] supplies the record for the restricted-ramification $G_{K,S}$, the embeddings of the finite places in $S$, the roots $D=\mu_n(K_S)$ and the original normalized trace *gsCompactInv*. Its assumptions include the prime-power witness, the indicated places above the prime, the odd or totally complex condition, and the ordinary degree-three vanishing for the roots used to define that trace. By the corestriction result the base construction gets degree-three corestriction surjectivity for each open subgroup. The record gives its scalar bijections for all opens below the one roots-fixing witness. *gsPoitouTateDualityHypotheses* applies the present theorem at each exact prime power, and its existing composition is the one in §18.3.24. The examples above check signs for the restriction interface. Their trace is the difference of the two local coordinates evaluated on an explicit cycle. In the number-field application the trace on a local boundary is instead the sum of the original local invariants. [A2, PoincarePairs.lean:127–232; RestrictedRamification/BaseMain.lean:23–64; RestrictedRamification/BaseDefs.lean:293–320; PoitouTate/ZmodToQZCompactCohomologyResMapOfLocalInv.lean:207–232; PoitouTate/PoitouTateNineTermGs.lean:23–69.]

**Remark on the classical range [Paper].** Tate's ICM Theorem 3.1 gives the finite localization-kernel pairing and exact sequence and credits Poitou's independent work. Milne II.2.3 supplies the compact long exact sequences from the shifted restriction cone, with modified archimedean terms. For the ring of $S$-integers and the locally constant sheaf for $M^\vee$, the place condition makes $n$ invertible, as required for the comparison in II.2.9 and the finite perfect pairing in II.3.3. The derived dual is the sheaf for $M$ by II.3.3(a) and evaluation into the double dual. The modified archimedean groups vanish for odd torsion or a totally complex field. The $\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z$-valued local-invariant trace on $n$-torsion agrees with the original $R$-valued trace after the embedding $[a]\mapsto a/n+\mathbb Z$. The classical arithmetic conclusion is a specialization of that duality. The fixed-exponent proof above expounds the Alpöge–Claude implementation and its explicit hypotheses on a compact group and restriction family. The source reading covers the sign, the enlargement and the degree steps, with the arithmetic supplies used at their named interfaces and community verification recorded separately [Community]. [Tate, ICM 1962, §3](https://math.stanford.edu/~conrad/BSDseminar/refs/TateICM.pdf) · [Milne, Arithmetic Duality Theorems, II §§2–3](https://www.jmilne.org/math/Books/ADTnot.pdf) · [Community verification](https://math.mit.edu/~drew/grh/) · [Source ledger](../sources/SOURCES.md#chapter-18-18325-fixed-exponent-compact-duality)
